# MEMORY.md — LogCoreOS Long-Term Memory

Stable knowledge about this project: design decisions, security rules, and lessons learned. Update this file when a decision is made that should survive session restarts.

---

## What LogCoreOS Is

LogCoreOS is a self-hosted, open-source, AI-native life operating system. It gives individuals and families a private Brain (Markdown + JSON files) that an AI layer can read and act on.

**Two products:**
- **LogCore Brain** (`brain/`) — Free and open source. Markdown + JSON files. Works with any AI.
- **LogCore App** (`app/`) — FastAPI backend + React frontend, installable as a PWA.

**End goal:** A working, deployed, self-hosted platform where users control all their data, no cloud required.

---

## Core Design Decisions

**Brain = filesystem, no database**
User data lives in `brain/USERS/{name}/` as Markdown and JSON files. No SQL, no ORM.
**Why:** Human-readable, AI-friendly, vendor-agnostic, fully portable. User can take the Brain folder anywhere and any AI can read it.
**How to apply:** Never propose storing user data in a database. If a new feature needs persistence, add a JSON file under the appropriate Brain path.

**Atomic writes always**
All Brain file writes use `write_json()` and `write_markdown()` from `services/file_service.py`. These use `tempfile.mkstemp` + `os.replace()` for POSIX atomic writes.
**Why:** Prevents partial writes from crashing the app or corrupting data.
**How to apply:** Never use `open(..., 'w')` directly for Brain files. Always use the file_service helpers.

**JWT JTI revocation**
Tokens carry a unique `jti` field. Logout revokes the JTI in memory (`_revoked_jtis`) and persists to `auth.json`. On startup, `_bootstrap_revoked_jtis()` reloads the blacklist from disk.
**Why:** Stateless JWTs can't be invalidated without a revocation mechanism. JTI blacklist gives us true logout.
**How to apply:** Never skip JTI checks. If modifying auth, ensure the JTI is checked in `get_current_user()`.

**Module system: frontend + backend must stay in sync**
`ALL_MODULES` in `app/frontend/src/lib/constants.js` defines the module registry. Each module `id` must exactly match the string used in `require_module(module_id)` on the backend. Modules with `nav: false` and no `to` field appear in the Admin → RolesCard for toggling but are hidden from the sidebar and mobile drawer — used for sub-features that gate content within a page rather than a full page route.
**Why:** Mismatch causes silent access failures or broken feature gating. `nav: false` modules must still be in `ALL_MODULE_IDS` in `features_service.py`.
**How to apply:** When adding a module, update both `constants.js` AND the backend router's `require_module()` call. Current IDs: `dashboard`, `tasks`, `goals`, `calendar`, `household`, `notes`, `journal`, `chat`, `automations`, `home_assistant`, `team`, `assets`, `finance`, `contacts`. (`home_assistant` renamed from `home` 2026-08-24 — id, directory, API prefix, and Brain folder all renamed together; see that date's Key Decisions Log entries for the full migration story. `automations_business` — a role-editor checkbox that never actually gated anything — was removed entirely, not renamed, during Automations' 2026-08-25 conversion; this line went stale for a day since that removal wasn't reflected here until Tasks' own conversion touched this same line. `tasks`/`calendar` both converted into `module_packages/` 2026-08-25 too, ids unchanged.)

Modules also carry a `workspace` field in `constants.js` that controls which workspace they appear in by default — `'personal'` hides a module in the business workspace, `'business'` hides it in the personal workspace, absent means visible in both. Current workspace assignments: `household`, `journal`, `home_assistant` → `personal`; `team`, `automations_business` → `business`. Layout.jsx enforces this on the frontend; the backend `require_module()` guard still applies regardless of workspace.

**Dynamic CORS reads hosting.json at request time**
`DynamicCORSMiddleware` in `main.py` reads `brain/hosting.json` at each request, not at startup.
**Why:** Allows the Admin → Hosting panel to take effect immediately without a container restart.
**How to apply:** Never cache the CORS origin in memory. Read `hosting_service.effective_domain_url()` per request.

**Runtime hosting config always wins over env vars**
`cookie_secure` and `trust_proxy_headers` can be set in `docker/.env` (defaults) but are overridden at runtime by `brain/hosting.json`. Always use `hosting_service.effective_cookie_secure()` and `effective_trust_proxy_headers()` — never read `settings.*` directly in request-serving code.

**Feature roles are separate from auth roles**
Auth roles (`admin`, `member`, `guest`) control who can access admin endpoints. Feature roles (custom, e.g. `cleaner`) control which app modules are visible. They are stored and managed independently. `guest` is the default feature role for new users (security default). `member` is the internal fallback when a feature role goes missing.

**A pool (`_household`/`_team`) is just another `user_name` to any already-generic per-user service function — resolve the pool name at the router boundary, not inside the service (2026-08-12)**
Adding a pool-owned SimpleFIN bank connection (Household/Team → Bank Connections → "Connect a joint family account") needed **zero changes** to `simplefin_service.py` — every function there (`claim_and_save`, `get_connection`, `set_mapping`, `sync_user`, …) already took a plain `user_name: str` and called `simplefin_path(user_name)` = `user_path(user_name) / "Finance" / "simplefin.json"`, with no check anywhere that the name belongs to a real registered account. Since `_household`/`_team` are real folders under `USERS/` (used this same way for pool Finance books, Tasks, Events already), passing `"_household"` as `user_name` just works — the only new code needed was in `routers/finance_banking.py`: a `pool: "household"|"team"` → pseudo-user-name resolver (`_pool_user()`) plus admin-gated routes that call the exact same service functions the per-user endpoints already call, and `resolve_target_store()`'s existing `store == "household"` branch already resolves to the pool's own books regardless of which name owns the connection.
**Why:** This is the same pattern the Finance/Tasks/Calendar pool stores already established — pseudo-users aren't a special case baked into service logic, they're an ordinary `user_name` value that happens not to correspond to an `auth.json` entry. Keeping service functions generic on a plain string (never internally calling `get_user_by_id`/assuming a real account) is what makes this free.
**How to apply:** Before adding pool-specific parameters or branches to a service function, check whether it already takes a bare `user_name`/`store_user` string with no real-user validation baked in — if so, a pool feature is usually just a router-level name-resolution problem, not a service-layer one. Conversely, if a service function *does* validate against `auth_service.get_user_by_id()` internally, that's a sign it was never meant to accept a pseudo-user and needs a deliberate decision, not just a passed-through string.

**Write-capable dashboard blocks call each target module's own existing endpoint directly — never a unified dashboard-owned write path (2026-08-05)**
The `status_button` dashboard block (change a task's status, archive a contact/asset, set an asset's select-field value) is the first write-capable block in a system that was otherwise entirely passive. It does **not** go through a new `/dashboards/.../action` endpoint. The frontend calls `tasksApi.update`/`contactsApi.archive`/`assetsApi.update` etc. straight from the browser's own session — the exact same calls the Tasks/Contacts/Assets pages themselves make.
**Why:** A unified write endpoint would have to either thinly re-wrap these calls (pure duplication, one more place to update whenever a module's own write rules change) or reimplement each module's access logic inline — and the thresholds genuinely differ per module (contacts require strict `edit`; assets require the stricter `can_manage`; tasks are implicitly scoped to the caller's own store with no cross-user concept at all). Calling the real endpoint directly means the click is gated by that module's own current rules for free, with the dashboard's own access model and its `share_underlying_data` toggle **not in the call path at all** — so there is no way for that read-only sharing concession to accidentally leak into a write capability. `services/dashboard_blocks/_actions.py`'s resolvers are display-only (they exist to label the button — a record's name, its current status — through the same read-through gate every other block uses); the click itself never touches that file.
**How to apply:** If a future block type needs to perform a write, resist building a generic "run this action" endpoint. Find the module's own existing endpoint (or add one there, gated by that module's own rules, if it doesn't exist) and call it directly from the block component. The one UX consequence to keep in mind: a shared viewer can see a button labeled using the owner's visibility (via `share_underlying_data`) but have the click itself legitimately fail, since it always resolves as their own identity — the button needs real error feedback, never a silent no-op.

**AI agent tools must resolve through the same sharing-aware service functions the HTTP API uses — a bare own-store call is a silent capability gap, not a smaller feature (2026-08-14)**
`agent_service.py`'s note tools (`list_notes`, `search_brain`, `create_note`/`update_note`/`delete_note`/`move_note`) all called plain, own-store-only `notes_service.py` functions, while `routers/notes.py` — what the Notes page itself uses — already called the sharing-aware `list_visible_notes()`/`find_note_store()`. The AI could not see or use anything shared with the user at all, and failed as if the item didn't exist rather than reporting a permission problem. Fixed by giving `notes_service.py` a small shared `meets(access, need)` helper (services must not import from routers in this codebase, so the shared logic lives in the service, not imported from the router) and routing every note tool through `find_note_store()`/`list_visible_notes()` first, mirroring the exact pattern `update_asset` already used: resolve → check access → return a structured error dict (never raise) on denial. Also added a `read_note` tool (there was no dedicated note-content-read tool before — the closest was `read_brain_file`, whose hard own-directory-only containment check was deliberately left untouched, since it's also the only thing guarding journal/memory/profile, none of which are shareable at all).
**Why:** A module's sharing model is enforced entirely at the router layer by convention in this codebase — there's no service-level guarantee that "own-store" and "sharing-aware" variants stay behaviorally equivalent, so an agent tool written against the wrong one silently regresses every sharing feature for that module specifically for AI interactions, while humans using the page see it working fine. This is an easy mistake to repeat: a new agent tool is usually written by finding "the function that does X" rather than by checking which of possibly two functions the real HTTP endpoint calls.
**How to apply:** Before writing or reviewing any new agent tool that reads/writes a module with a sharing/pool concept (Notes, Assets, Contacts, Finance), grep the equivalent router file first and confirm the tool calls the same sharing-aware entry point, not merely a same-named service function. If the module's `meets()`-equivalent access-order check doesn't exist yet as a shared service helper, add one rather than inlining the comparison in the tool handler.

**A cross-book Finance Transfer is two ordinary transactions linked by `transfer_pair_id` + denormalized peer fields, not a new first-class object (2026-08-14)**
`finance_service.py` transactions gained `transfer_pair_id` (uuid, null unless this row is one leg of a transfer) plus denormalized peer info (`transfer_peer_book_id/name`, `transfer_peer_account_id/name`, `transfer_peer_workspace`) rather than a new stored "transfer" entity. `POST /finance/transfers` calls the existing `add_transaction()` twice (once per book/workspace) — same currency required, edit access required on both books, cross-workspace allowed (including personal ↔ business). Deliberately skips `finance_planning_service.on_transactions_added()` (the router-level bill-matching/budget-alert sweep) for both legs, since `match_bill()`'s matching is category-blind and could otherwise false-match a transfer leg to an unrelated recurring bill. `monthly_report()`/`pnl()`/`budget_status()` each filter out any transaction with `transfer_pair_id` set before summing. The old single-tx `PATCH`/`DELETE /finance/books/{book_id}/transactions/{tx_id}` now 409 if the target has `transfer_pair_id` set — a transfer's two legs can only be edited/deleted together, via the dedicated transfer endpoints, which take both book_ids explicitly rather than needing a new cross-book index.
**Why:** `add_transaction()` builds its stored row from an explicit named-field allowlist, not `**kwargs` passthrough — this is why the new transfer fields had to be added there explicitly (caught before any code was written: they'd have been silently dropped otherwise). Denormalizing the peer's book/account/workspace onto each leg (rather than requiring a join/lookup) mirrors this codebase's existing convention (dashboards' `_template_label`, by-asset transaction lookups carrying `book_id`/`book_name`) and means the frontend never needs a pair-id-only lookup to render a transfer row or build a PATCH/DELETE call.
**How to apply:** When extending `add_transaction()`'s stored shape again, remember the allowlist and add new fields there explicitly. When adding any new report/aggregate that sums transaction amounts, filter out `transfer_pair_id`-tagged rows the same way `monthly_report`/`pnl`/`budget_status` do, or a transfer will double-count as both an expense and income in that new aggregate.

**Contact editor/detail views branch whole sections on `contact.type === 'company'`, following the existing `isSelf`-gated section precedent (2026-08-14)**
`type: 'person'|'company'` already existed as a real, validated field (`CONTACT_TYPES`) — what was missing was any field-*visibility* branching by it; every contact rendered the same ~40-field template regardless of type. `ContactModal.jsx`/`ContactDetail.jsx` now gate whole sections: Personal + Career are person-only; Locations (new `locations: list[dict]`) + Hours (new `hours: list[dict]`, always 7 entries, Mon–Sun) are company-only; Family is relabeled "Affiliated People" for a company and drops marital_status/pets (the link-a-related-contact mechanism itself is unchanged for both). The admin custom-fields system was deliberately NOT used for Locations/Hours — it's a single global list of 5 scalar types with no per-type scoping and no repeatable/array type, so genuinely new structured fields still follow the `career_history` precedent (a hand-rolled `list[dict]` + its own dedicated validator) instead.
**Why:** The `isSelf`-gated section pattern already in `ContactModal.jsx` was the closest existing precedent for "render a whole block only when a condition on the contact is true" — reusing that shape (rather than per-field ternaries scattered throughout the form) keeps the person/company split legible as the contact schema keeps growing. A self-contact's `type` was previously only defaulted to `"person"` at creation and never actually protected afterward — added a guard (`{!isSelf}` on the toggle client-side, `_validate_contact()`/`update_contact()` server-side) so a self-contact can't be flipped to `"company"` after the fact, since the whole private-fields/edit-access model for "your own contact" assumes person.
**How to apply:** Any new contact field that only makes sense for one type belongs in a type-gated section following this same shape, not a field that's simply left blank for the other type. If a field needs to be a repeatable structured list (not a scalar), don't reach for the admin custom-fields system — follow the `career_history`/`locations`/`hours` hand-rolled-list-plus-validator pattern instead. Deliberately deferred, not built this pass: the "Employer" picker inside `CareerHistoryEditor` doesn't filter to company-type contacts and hardcodes `type: 'person'` on quick-create even there — logged in `docs/TASKS.md`.

**Dashboard action buttons are a generic, user-built `actions[]` config array resolved by the block's own `shape`, not a per-block-type special case (2026-08-15)**
Any block config can now carry `actions: [{id, kind: 'nav'|'status', ...}]`, authored via a new `ActionsEditor` repeater in `BlockPicker.jsx` (modeled on `LocationsEditor`'s add/remove/edit shape) whenever `BLOCK_REGISTRY[type].recordKind` is set. Render behavior is driven entirely by the block's existing `shape` field (added 2026-08-07, see the Key Decisions Log entry below): `shape: 'list'` blocks render the configured actions once per row (substituting that row's own record id); `shape: 'detail'` blocks render them once against the block's single configured record. One rule covers both of the owner's named examples (a Notes block button that opens that note; an Assets block button that updates status) with zero new per-module branches. `BlockActionButtons`/`ActionButton`/`runStatusAction` in `blocks.jsx` reuse `nav_button`/`status_button`'s exact existing click logic — no new write path, following the standing rule above ("Write-capable dashboard blocks call each target module's own existing endpoint directly"). `block.config` (including `actions`) already shipped to the frontend in the render response before this change (`render.py` ~line 126) — no backend resolver changes were needed for the mechanism itself, only a new `contacts_list` block type (there was no general "list of contacts" block before this) and a factored-out reusable helper in `_actions.py`.
**Why:** A per-block-type special case (e.g. "Notes blocks get an open button, Assets blocks get a status button") would need new code for every future module; keying off `shape` instead means any future list-shaped or detail-shaped block gets the same capability automatically once it declares a `recordKind`. `pool_tasks` deliberately has **no** `recordKind` despite being list-shaped — its rows route through `sharedApi`/`teamApi` by `_source`, not `tasksApi`, and its deep-link lookup only searches `taskList`, not `assignedPoolTasks` — so a generic task action button would silently target the wrong API or fail to resolve.
**How to apply:** A new block type gets action-button support for free by setting `recordKind` in `BLOCK_REGISTRY` (`task`/`asset`/`contact`/`event`/`note`, see `actionKinds.js`) — only do this if every row's record genuinely resolves through that module's own standard API by plain id; if a block's rows are sourced through an indirect/pooled path (like `pool_tasks`), leave `recordKind` unset and explain why in a comment, don't add a special case to make it fit.

**Chat conversations are tracked by a workspace-scoped session index (`agent/chat_sessions.json`), and `POST /chat` marks a session "running" before the model call so a crash can't leave it stuck (2026-08-15)**
Each conversation now has a stable `chat_id` (assigned on first send, not deferred to autosave). `agent_service.py`'s `load_sessions`/`get_session`/`upsert_session`/`mark_session_read`/`delete_session_by_filename` manage a capped (`_SESSIONS_CAP = 50`) per-user-per-workspace index; each entry tracks `status` (`idle`/`running`/`awaiting_approval`/`awaiting_answer`), `unread`, `updated_at`, and a message preview. `routers/chat.py`'s `chat()` handler computes `effective_workspace` once (right after `resume_payload` resolution) and threads it through every session call — the session functions take `workspace` explicitly and use `ws_path()`, not `user_path()`, because chat archives themselves live under `ws_path(...)/Chats/`; an un-scoped index could otherwise point one `chat_id` at two different physical files if the workspace changed mid-conversation (caught during implementation, before writing tests). The handler marks the session `running` before calling `run_agent()`, wraps the call in try/except to reset it to `idle` on an unhandled exception, and calls `notify_user()` unconditionally on success — this is also the mechanism the frontend's Chats drawer uses for its active/unread indicators, and doubles as the "last opened chat" pointer (most-recent `updated_at` the user actually opened), so no separate last-opened-chat file was needed.
**Why:** `POST /chat` stayed fully synchronous (not `BackgroundTasks`/`asyncio.create_task`) for this pass — no `AbortController` exists anywhere in the frontend, so an in-flight request is never client-cancelled, and Starlette doesn't auto-cancel a handler on disconnect by default; going async would have meant solving orphaned-run cleanup as a new problem. Instead, "background" is satisfied by the session index alone: switching to a different (or new) conversation while one is `running` doesn't block the UI, since `Chat.jsx` keys its message state by `chat_id` and only the currently-open conversation's history needs to be loaded.
**How to apply:** Any future feature that needs "what's the state of a chat right now" (badges, notifications, resuming) should read `chat_sessions.json` via these helpers, not re-derive state from scanning saved chat archive files. If `POST /chat` is ever made genuinely async (fire-and-forget), the "mark running before the call, reset on exception" pattern here is the one to keep — it's what makes a crashed run visibly `idle`-again rather than stuck `running` forever.

**A reopened chat re-fetches its live pending-turn card from `pending_turns.json` by `chat_id` — the `.md` archive itself only ever stores plain text (2026-08-15)**
`pending_turns.json` records (already saved whenever a turn pauses — see the pause/resume mechanism, 2026-08-09) now carry a `chat_id` field (`run_agent()` gained a `chat_id` param, threaded from `routers/chat.py`'s `req.chat_id` into all three `save_pending_turn()` call sites). New `agent_service.get_pending_turn_by_chat_id(user_name, chat_id)` and `GET /chat/pending/{chat_id}` look it up and return `{run_id, mode, steps}` (`mode` here is the *pause status* — `awaiting_approval`/`awaiting_answer`, derived from `pending["kind"]` — not the chat mode approve/plan/auto/research the pending record's own `mode` field holds; these are two different concepts that happen to share a confusingly similar name, see the router function's own comment). `Chat.jsx`'s `openSession()` calls this whenever a session's own `status` (from `GET /chat/sessions`) is `awaiting_approval`/`awaiting_answer`, and re-attaches `steps`/`mode`/`runId` onto the last loaded message — exactly the shape a live pause response already carries, so `ApprovalCard`/`AskUserQuestionCard`/`ProposalCard` render identically either way.
**Why:** The saved `.md` archive (`_write_chat_archive`) always did (and still does) get the assistant's pause-prompt text written to it (`result["final_answer"]`, e.g. "I need your approval to make these changes.") — so reopening a paused conversation never lost the *text*. What it lost was the *interactive card* (and the `run_id` needed to act on it) entirely, since the archive has no structured step data — a real, reported bug (a paused approval/question/plan effectively vanished on reload with no way to act on it except starting over). The data needed to fix this already existed in `pending_turns.json`; the only gap was that nothing could look it up by `chat_id` (it was only ever looked up by `run_id`, known only within the same live session).
**How to apply:** Any future feature that needs to know "does this specific conversation currently have something awaiting the user" should read `chat_sessions.json`'s `status` field (already the source of truth for that) and, if the interactive content itself is needed, follow through to `get_pending_turn_by_chat_id()` — don't try to reconstruct card state from the `.md` archive, which was never meant to hold it.

**A same-book Finance Transfer's two legs are indistinguishable by `transfer_pair_id` alone — the lookup must also disambiguate by amount sign (2026-08-15)**
`finance_service.get_transaction_by_transfer_pair(store_user, workspace, book_id, transfer_pair_id)` used to return the *first* transaction matching the pair id in that book. For a cross-book transfer this was fine (each book only ever holds one of the two legs) — but for a **same-book** transfer (two accounts, one book), both legs share the exact same `store_user`/`workspace`/`book_id`, live in the same shard file, and share the same `transfer_pair_id` — so looking it up "for the from side" and "for the to side" returned the **identical transaction** both times. `finance_transfers.py`'s `delete_transfer`/`update_transfer` then operated on that one transaction twice (once under each name), leaving the real second leg completely untouched — a real, demonstrated bug (a same-book transfer delete silently removed only one side; a same-book update silently orphaned the other leg's stale amount). Fixed by adding a required `negative: bool` keyword arg to `get_transaction_by_transfer_pair()` — `add_transaction()` already always stores the "from" leg negative and the "to" leg positive, so sign is a reliable discriminator regardless of whether the two legs happen to share a book.
**Why:** This bug was invisible to the *existing* test suite because `test_finance_transfers.py`'s delete/update tests only ever exercised a cross-book (`personal_book`/`business_book`) pair — the one scenario where "first match" happens to be correct by accident, since there's only one candidate per book. Added `test_same_book_transfer_delete_removes_both_legs`/`test_same_book_transfer_update_moves_both_legs` (two-account single-book fixture) specifically to close this coverage gap; also confirmed via a full live browser click-through (create → delete) in an isolated instance, checking both books' transaction lists directly via the API afterward.
**How to apply:** Any future lookup keyed by a *shared* id across a pair/group of records (not a record's own unique id) must consider whether two members of that group could plausibly resolve to the same underlying store/scope — if so, "first match" is a latent bug waiting for exactly that overlap case, not a safe simplification. Test the same-scope-overlap case explicitly, not just the general case.

**Any uncaught exception now returns real JSON, not Starlette's default plain-text 500 — a global exception handler in `main.py` (2026-08-15)**
`@app.exception_handler(Exception)` catches anything that escapes a route handler and returns `{"detail": "Internal server error"}` as real `application/json`, logging the actual exception server-side first. Previously, an uncaught exception fell through to Starlette's `ServerErrorMiddleware` default: a **plain-text** body (`"Internal Server Error"`). The frontend's `api.js::request()` calls `res.json()` unconditionally on every non-401/204 response — feeding that plain-text body into `JSON.parse()` throws, and **Safari/WebKit's parse-error message for non-JSON input is its own opaque `"The string did not match the expected pattern."`**, surfaced verbatim as the error with zero indication anything server-side went wrong. Found via a real bug: `push_service.py::send_push()`'s JWT-building/payload-encryption calls sat outside its own try/except, so a malformed subscription could throw uncaught, straight through `POST /push/test`, into exactly this failure mode. `send_push()` itself is now also fully exception-safe (the try/except now wraps the whole body, not just the final `urlopen` call) — the global handler is a second, general layer, not a replacement for handling expected failure modes explicitly where they're known. `api.js::request()` also got a defensive `try { res.json() } catch` fallback, in case a *non-app* non-JSON body ever reaches the client (a reverse proxy's own error page, for instance) — belt and suspenders, not redundant with the backend fix, since that path is reachable even when this app's own code is fine.
**How to apply:** Don't rely on "the caller will show a sensible error" for an uncaught exception — Safari in particular turns a non-JSON error body into a genuinely misleading message. When a service function's failure needs to surface as a specific, actionable HTTP error (like `push.py`'s 400-vs-502 distinction), catch it explicitly and raise `HTTPException` with a real `detail` — the global handler is the backstop for everything *else*, not a reason to skip that.

**REVERTED same-day: splitting `<main>`'s padding onto an inner wrapper to fix the scrollbar overlap broke Chat's composer and spread the keyboard-layout bug into Notes (2026-08-15)**
The scrollbar-edge fix below (padding split into an inner `<div>`, bottom padding staying on `<main>`) looked correct on paper — verified by reasoning through `sticky`'s containing-block rules before shipping — but broke in real mobile Safari: reported as "ai chats text entry box and all that is broken now" plus the *same* keyboard-not-rising-above-it symptom newly appearing in Notes, a page this change never directly touched. The shared regression point was `Layout.jsx`'s `<main>` itself: the extra nested flex level (`<main class="flex flex-col"> > <div class="flex-1 min-h-0 flex flex-col"> > <Outlet/>`) didn't cascade `min-h-0` through both levels the same way in real mobile Safari as it appeared to during review here. **Reverted in full** — `<main>` is back to the single-element version with its own padding directly on it, and Chat's composer is back to a plain `shrink-0` flow child (see below). The scrollbar-overlap cosmetic issue itself is deferred, not solved, rather than risking a third round of this.
**How to apply:** A CSS fix reasoned through carefully (even correctly, per spec) is still unverified until it's actually seen working on the real target device — this project's own no-real-iOS-device constraint means layout changes to *shared* chrome (`Layout.jsx`, anything every page renders through) carry outsized blast radius if wrong, since a bug there doesn't stay contained to the page being worked on. Prefer the smallest possible change to shared layout, and treat "no device to verify on" as a reason to lean conservative there specifically, more than in a single page's own component.

**REVERTED same-day: `sticky bottom-0` for the Chat composer, part of the same broken change above (2026-08-15)**
Also reverted back to a plain `shrink-0 pt-2` flow child. The keyboard-avoidance problem itself (composer not rising above the on-screen keyboard) remains **unfixed** as of this entry — two attempts (the original `visualViewport`-based JS correction, and this `sticky` CSS attempt) have not resolved it, and the second attempt introduced a worse regression. Not re-attempting a third fix in the same session without a way to verify on a real device first.
**How to apply:** If revisiting this, verify on a real iOS device (or get the owner to test a specific build) before considering it done — this problem class has now burned two attempts on reasoning-without-verification.

**`backdrop-filter` (Tailwind's `backdrop-blur-*`) creates a new containing block for `position: fixed` descendants — a full-screen overlay nested inside one gets trapped inside it (2026-08-15)**
`assetDisplay.jsx`'s `ImageLightbox` (opened from an asset attachment thumbnail) used a plain `fixed inset-0` overlay and appeared trapped inside the small card it opened from instead of covering the screen. Root cause: both real call sites — `AssetView.jsx` inside `AssetModal`'s own `.modal-card`, and a dashboard block's `.card` wrapper — have `backdrop-blur-sm` (`backdrop-filter: blur(...)`) on an ancestor. Per the CSS spec, `backdrop-filter` (like `transform`/`filter`/`perspective`/`contain: paint`) creates a new containing block for `position: fixed` descendants, so the "fixed" overlay resolved against that blurred ancestor's box instead of the true viewport. Fixed by rendering `ImageLightbox` through a React `createPortal` straight onto `document.body` — the first portal usage anywhere in this codebase — which escapes the DOM tree (and every ancestor's CSS) entirely, so this can't recur regardless of which container ends up embedding the thumbnail component in the future. Separately, the lightbox was also reusing `.modal-overlay`'s `items-end md:items-center` (a deliberate bottom-sheet-on-mobile treatment for *forms*) — a square/non-tall image then sat in the bottom half of the screen on mobile instead of centered; fixed by giving the lightbox its own always-`items-center` overlay instead of reusing that class.
**How to apply:** Before nesting any `position: fixed` full-screen element (a lightbox, a global toast, anything meant to cover the true viewport) inside existing app chrome, check whether any ancestor has `backdrop-filter`/`filter`/`transform`/`perspective`/`contain` — if reachable from more than one place in the tree (like a shared thumbnail component), the safest default is a portal to `document.body`, not an assumption about what the current call site's ancestors look like. Don't reuse `.modal-overlay` for anything that isn't a form-like dialog — its mobile bottom-sheet alignment is a deliberate, scoped choice for that pattern, not a general "full-screen overlay" default.

**The Chat "last opened conversation" pointer is workspace-scoped in localStorage (`lc_chat_last_id_${workspace}`), and a `useEffect([workspace])` still fires once on mount even when nothing changed (2026-08-15, two compounding bugs)**
Chat.jsx's workspace-switch effect (`useEffect(..., [workspace])`, added 2026-08-15 for background multi-chat) unconditionally reset `chatId`/`messages` — but a dependency-array `useEffect` also fires once after the very first render, not only on later changes, so this reset ran on **every mount**, wiping out whatever the mount effect had just restored before it ever reached the screen. Fixed with a `useRef`-based first-run guard (`isFirstWorkspaceRun`), the standard pattern for "only run on a real change, not the initial mount." Separately, the restore key itself was global (`lc_chat_last_id`, no workspace suffix) — switching from personal to business and back always landed on a blank chat rather than each workspace's own last conversation, since a chat_id/session belongs to exactly one workspace's Chats/ folder. Fixed by keying the localStorage pointer per workspace and extracting the mount effect's restore logic into a shared `restoreForWorkspace(ws)` function, called both on mount and from the (now-guarded) workspace-switch effect.
**How to apply:** Any `useEffect` whose body should only run on a *change*, not the initial mount, needs an explicit first-run guard — a dependency array alone doesn't provide this. Any localStorage pointer to "the last X the user had open" that's meaningful per-workspace (chat, or any future per-workspace last-active-record pattern) needs the workspace baked into the key, not a single global key.

**`autoFocus` is a mount-time-only DOM attribute — it does not re-fire when a later prop/state change would otherwise "deserve" focus again (2026-08-15)**
`TransactionModal.jsx`'s Amount field autofocuses on a new Expense/Income entry (a deliberate "start typing immediately" convenience) via `autoFocus={!editing}`. Switching the same open modal's kind toggle to Transfer left that field — and the on-screen numeric keyboard on mobile — focused the whole time, covering the book/account picker fields the user actually needed next, since `autoFocus` only ever applies once, at the moment the DOM node is first inserted; it has no effect on a later re-render with different props. Fixed two ways: gated the initial `autoFocus` itself (`!editing && kind !== 'transfer'`, covering the rare case of a modal opening already in Transfer mode) and added an explicit `useEffect` that calls `.blur()` via a ref whenever `kind` changes to `'transfer'` (covering the common case: default kind is Expense, user switches after the modal's already open).
**How to apply:** `autoFocus` alone is never sufficient for "this field should be focused whenever condition X is true" if X can become true *after* mount — pair it with an explicit ref + effect for the on-prop-change case, the same shape used here.

**A resume's history array correctly ends in the triggering `user` message, not `assistant` — `sendResume` was using the wrong trim helper, silently corrupting the saved archive on every single approval (2026-08-15, real production bug: "chat breaks after accepting an approval card")**
When a turn pauses, `messages.slice(1, -1)` at that moment always ends with the *user* message that triggered the still-pending assistant reply — a normal alternating conversation always has a user turn directly before the assistant turn currently pending. `Chat.jsx`'s `sendResume()` was feeding this into `toApiHistory()`, built for `send()`'s different shape — its trailing-pop step (`while (h.length && h[h.length-1].role !== 'assistant') h.pop()`) assumed a dangling non-assistant entry was leftover cruft, so it silently discarded that real, current user message on *every* resume. The server then archived `history + [resumed_reply]` with that user turn missing, leaving two assistant entries adjacent in the saved `.md` file. This was invisible within the same live session (local `messages` state stayed internally consistent, per the 2026-08-09 in-place-replace fix) — the corruption only surfaced once that archive was *reloaded* into a fresh `messages` array (a page remount, switching workspaces and back, reopening from the Chats drawer), at which point the very next real send correctly 422'd (`ChatRequest`'s alternation validator caught the malformed pair) with "History message N has unexpected role 'assistant'". This bug was latent before 2026-08-15's background-multi-chat work, but that work made reloads (and therefore hitting it) dramatically more frequent — every mount/workspace-switch now restores the last conversation, where before this only happened on an explicit "open saved chat" action.
**The fix has two halves, both required:** (1) new `toResumeHistory()` in `Chat.jsx` — same leading-trim as `toApiHistory()`, but deliberately *no* trailing-pop, since ending in `user` is exactly correct here. (2) `ChatRequest.validate_history_alternates()` in `routers/chat.py` now only enforces "must end with an assistant message" when `self.resume` is *not* set — a resume's history is expected to end in `user`. Fixing only the frontend half would have made every resume 422 outright (the validator unconditionally rejected a `user`-ending history before this); fixing only the backend half would have left the archive-corruption bug fully intact (the frontend was still sending the wrong, truncated history).
**How to apply:** Never reuse a "trim this array to a valid shape" helper across two call sites whose *correct* shape genuinely differs — `send()` and `sendResume()` look superficially similar (both send a `history` array to the same endpoint) but have opposite correct trailing-role expectations. When a bug's real trigger is "reload stale/corrupted data written earlier," the reproduction needs to go through an actual save→reload cycle, not just the live in-session path — this is exactly the kind of bug a live-session-only test suite (the existing tests before this fix) will never catch, hence the new `test_resume_then_followup_send_does_not_break_history_alternation` regression test, which explicitly re-reads the archive and replays a fresh follow-up send against it.

**A stale-until-pinged "presence" file gates chat completion notifications — no new real-time infra, since presence only needs to be approximately right (2026-08-15, owner ask: "only send a notification... when the user is not on that module")**
`POST /chat` is a synchronous request/response with no live channel back to the server once it returns, so the server has no inherent way to know "is the user still looking at this conversation" by the time a background/multi-step run finishes. Added `agent/chat_presence.json` (single most-recent `{chat_id, seen_at}` per user, not workspace-scoped, mirroring `pending_turns.json`'s own shape choice) — `POST /chat/presence` (`agent_service.record_chat_presence`) lets the client say "I'm looking at this chat_id right now"; `agent_service.is_chat_present(user, chat_id)` is true only if that ping is younger than `_PRESENCE_STALE_AFTER_SECONDS` (45s). `Chat.jsx` pings on mount/`chatId` change and every 20s while `document.visibilityState === 'visible'`, via `setInterval` + a `visibilitychange` listener — deliberately **no explicit "I left" signal**; presence just goes stale on its own once the interval stops firing (tab closed, backgrounded — mobile browsers throttle/pause timers in a backgrounded tab, which conveniently makes presence go stale *faster* exactly when it should). `routers/chat.py`'s completion handler checks presence once, right before both the `unread` flag and the `notify_user()` call — both are skipped together when the user is still there, since a conversation you're actively watching complete isn't meaningfully "unread" either.
**Why not a real-time channel (WebSocket/SSE) instead:** no such infra exists anywhere in this codebase today, and adding one just for a notification-suppression nicety would be a disproportionate new dependency — a polled, stale-after-a-fixed-window file is consistent with the Brain's own filesystem-as-database philosophy and is good enough here, where an occasional false positive (one redundant notification sent) is a much smaller cost than real-time infrastructure would be to justify.
**How to apply:** Any future "does the server need to know if the client is still watching" feature should reuse this same pattern (a small per-user JSON file, a lightweight ping endpoint, a staleness window) rather than reaching for a persistent connection — this app has consistently chosen polling/staleness-window patterns over real-time infra elsewhere (the update-watcher's `pending_update` flag file, the AI usage cap's periodic checks) and this is the same shape again.
**Generalized app-wide, 2026-08-17**: `services/presence_service.py` (`record_presence`/`is_online`, 90s staleness) is this exact pattern lifted out of Chat specifically — one `presence.json` per user (not a shared multi-writer file, so no `update_json()` concurrency concern), pinged by `Layout.jsx` on every page (not just Chat) every 30s while visible. Built as infrastructure for an online/offline dot on user-linked Contacts (self-contacts) — the dot itself isn't wired up yet (pending the Contacts cross-workspace rework), but the ping/read mechanism is live and independent of that UI landing. Kept deliberately separate from `chat_presence.json` (conversation-scoped, different question — "is anyone watching *this chat*" vs. "is this *user* online at all") rather than merged, since collapsing them would make the chat-notification-suppression check fire on any-page activity instead of actually watching that conversation.

**The agent can read every block's grid coordinates and move/resize an existing one — position control followed size control the same day, once `get_dashboard` could actually show it where things are (2026-08-15, owner ask: "a coordinate system that the agent can read... move them and reshape them to suitable spots")**
Earlier the same day, `add_dashboard_block`/`update_dashboard_block` gained a `layout: {w?, h?}` override (size only) — position was deliberately withheld because the agent had no visibility into what else was on the grid, so choosing x/y would have been a blind guess. That gap is now closed: `get_dashboard`'s response includes a top-level `grid: {cols: 36, row_px: 24}` and each block's own `layout: {x, y, w, h}` (desktop/`lg` only — the one breakpoint the agent can meaningfully reason about; mobile is always full-width and its own stacking order is left alone). `update_dashboard_block`'s `layout` override now also accepts `x`/`y`, clamped by the same `_apply_layout_override()`/`_clamp_block_layout_override()` (`agent_service.py`) — `x` is bounded against the block's *resulting* width (its new `w` if the same call also resizes it, otherwise its current `w`) so it can never end up hanging off the right edge of the 36-column grid. `add_dashboard_block` still never accepts position — new blocks always auto-stack via `stacked_layout()` — a stray `x`/`y` in its `layout` input (its schema doesn't declare them, but nothing stops a model from sending extra keys) is silently ignored since `allow_position=False` there.
**The real bug this design caught in its own test:** an x-only move (no accompanying `w`) initially clamped against the *full* grid width instead of the block's actual current width, because the clamp function's fallback for "no new w given" defaulted to `_LG_COLS` (36) rather than the block's real, possibly-much-narrower current width — silently pinning `x` to 0 for any block not already full-width. Caught by `test_update_dashboard_block_can_move_position` before shipping, fixed by threading the current width through as `current_w` so the fallback is the block's own existing size, not the whole grid.
**Why still no collision prevention:** the goal was giving the agent the same information a person dragging a block on screen can already see (what's there, roughly where), not building real occupancy-aware placement logic server-side — the tool descriptions explicitly tell the model to call `get_dashboard` first and reason about the returned coordinates itself. `react-grid-layout`'s own `preventCollision={true}` only applies to interactive human dragging in the browser; nothing enforces non-overlap on the stored data either way, so an agent write that ignores the coordinates it was just given can still produce an overlap — same as it already could before this feature, just now avoidable rather than unavoidable.
**How to apply:** Any future agent capability that needs to reason about existing state before acting (not just apply a bounded override) should follow this same shape — expose the relevant read data through the tool the model is already told to call first, rather than trying to enforce correctness purely through backend validation.

---

## Security Rules

1. **Never trust user input as file paths.** Always resolve through `user_path()` from `file_service.py`.
2. **Brain file content injected into AI prompts must be wrapped in `<brain_data>` XML tags** to prevent prompt injection. See `routers/chat.py:_safe()`.
3. **`trust_proxy_headers`** defaults to `False`. Only enable when there is a trusted reverse proxy in front of the app.
4. **`SECRET_KEY`** must be changed from the default before any network exposure. `launch.sh` generates one automatically. As of 2026-07-20 the app **refuses to start** (`_startup_checks()` → `sys.exit(1)`) when `SECRET_KEY` is the `change-me-in-production` default or empty, unless `ALLOW_INSECURE_SECRET_KEY=true` (local-dev escape hatch only). Fresh installs are also secure-by-default now: `launch.sh` writes `COOKIE_SECURE=true` and empty `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` (was `false`/`*`); existing `.env` files are left untouched.
4a. **`ALLOWED_ORIGINS`** must not be a literal `"*"` on any network-exposed instance. As of 2026-08-12 the app **refuses to start** (`_startup_checks()` → `sys.exit(1)`) on wildcard CORS, mirroring rule 4 exactly, unless `ALLOW_INSECURE_CORS=true` (local-dev escape hatch only, undocumented in `.env.example` on purpose — same treatment as `ALLOW_INSECURE_SECRET_KEY`). Every real deployment binds non-loopback (the Docker image always runs `uvicorn --host 0.0.0.0`), so this was never just a theoretical LAN-only risk. **Existing installs that never explicitly set `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` and still have the old `*` default in `docker/.env` will refuse to start on next restart/update** until they set a real origin or the escape hatch — the same accepted tradeoff as the SECRET_KEY check. An empty string (the value `launch.sh` writes on fresh installs) is not `"*"` and is unaffected. **2026-08-15: this bit a real existing instance on its first relaunch since the guard shipped** — `launch.sh` only ever wrote `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` inside `generate_env()`, which is skipped whenever `docker/.env` already exists, so no already-deployed instance had any path to picking up a real value; the app just went down with a generic health-check timeout and no indication why. Fixed by adding `migrate_insecure_cors()` to both `launch.sh` (before `launch_containers`) and `docker/update.sh` (top of `do_update()`, before the fetch/rebuild): if `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` is still `*`, auto-set it from `brain/hosting.json`'s `domain_url` (same source `sync_tunnel_token()` already trusts) when one's on file, else print the actual cause instead of a generic timeout. `update.sh`'s existing rollback-on-failed-health-check already kept an auto-updating instance from actually going *down* during this — but it also meant the instance silently kept re-installing the same old pre-hardening commit forever rather than ever landing the real update, since the health check would fail every time. **Residual gap, stated plainly:** an instance already stuck in that rollback loop still needs one manual `git pull && bash launch.sh` (or a one-line `docker/.env` edit) to break out — the fix has to actually land before it can apply itself, an unavoidable chicken-and-egg for a self-healing migration shipped in the same release as the thing it's healing.
5. **Passwords** are bcrypt-hashed. Never store or log plaintext passwords.
6. **`brain/_system/auth.json`, `brain/ai_settings.json`, and `docker/.env` must never be committed.** All are in `.gitignore` (`ai_settings.json` was missing until 2026-07-06 — caught when the live API key showed up as untracked).
7. **`cookie_secure`** should be `true` in any HTTPS deployment. The Admin → Hosting panel sets this at runtime.
8. **Self-hosters must never see the Infisical card** in Admin unless a token was configured at deploy time. Env var tokens cannot be cleared via UI — only file-sourced tokens can be cleared.
9. **Rate limits are enforced on all endpoints.** Login: 5/5 min; register: 3/hour; general reads: 30/min; writes: 10/min; admin ops: 20/min. Always apply `rate_limit(count, window_secs)` to new endpoints. `rate_limit()` takes an optional `bucket=` name so several paths can share one budget — `/auth/login` and `/auth/token` share `bucket="auth-login"` because they run the same credential check (added 2026-07-20; without it the two paths gave an attacker 2× the login attempts).
9b. **Signed auto-updates (opt-in)** (`docker/update.sh`, added 2026-07-20): the updater `fetch`es → `verify_signature()` → `merge --ff-only` (no blind `git pull`). Set `UPDATE_REQUIRE_SIGNATURE=true` in `docker/.env` to refuse any incoming commit that isn't GPG-signed (commit or annotated tag) by a trusted keyring key — hardens against a compromised origin. Default off (unsigned deploys keep working); enabling requires signing release commits/tags and importing the signing pubkey into the updater's keyring. `update.sh` now sources `docker/.env` at startup for this.
9a. **Account-scoped login lockout** (`auth_service.login_attempt()` + `_failed_logins`, added 2026-07-20): a second layer *underneath* the per-IP limiter, keyed on the account email, to stop distributed credential-stuffing (many IPs, one account). After `_LOCKOUT_THRESHOLD` (10) failed attempts within `_LOCKOUT_WINDOW` (15 min) the account is temporarily refused (routers return 429) until the oldest failure ages out. It's a **sliding-window temporary lock, never permanent**, and the routers check the lock *before* authenticating so attempts made while locked don't extend it — this deliberately prevents a victim-lockout DoS. In-memory only (resets on restart), like the rate limiter; single-worker uvicorn. Both `/auth/login` and `/auth/token` go through `login_attempt()`, which returns `(user, lock_remaining)` — never call bare `authenticate()` from a login route or you lose the lockout.
10. **Security headers are added by `SecurityHeadersMiddleware`** in `main.py`: `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin`, `Permissions-Policy` (camera/mic/geo/etc. all off), `Cache-Control: no-store` on all `/api/*` responses, and `Strict-Transport-Security` **only** on HTTPS requests (see `_is_https_request()` — never sent on a plain-HTTP LAN instance, which would lock the browser into HTTPS-only). No CSP yet (deferred — the SPA loads external Google Fonts, so a policy needs testing first).
10a. **CSP is set by `SecurityHeadersMiddleware`** (`_CSP` in `main.py`, added 2026-07-20): `script-src 'self'` (all JS is bundled — the inline SW-registration script was moved from `index.html` into `main.jsx` specifically so no `'unsafe-inline'` is needed for scripts), `style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline' https://fonts.googleapis.com` (React sets `style=""` attributes for dynamic colors/backgrounds — style attrs can't take a nonce, so inline styles are unavoidable), `font-src` allows `fonts.gstatic.com`. **Do not re-add an inline `<script>` to `index.html`** — it will be CSP-blocked. Verified against a live `/login` load (zero violations).
10b. **Automation asset READ is pool-only** — `_automation_store(..., read_only=True)` (assets.py) rejects arbitrary users so a leaked automation token can't bulk-export one user's assets. Writes may still target a specific user. Mirrors the Contacts automation API's write-only design (2026-07-20).
11a. **`authenticate()` is constant-time** — an unknown email still runs a bcrypt verify against `auth_service._DUMMY_HASH` so login timing can't enumerate accounts (2026-07-20).
11b. **Automation-inbox `url` is scheme-restricted to `http(s)`** at the Pydantic boundary (`InboxItemIn`) and guarded again in `Automations.jsx` — it's rendered as a clickable link in a reviewing admin's origin, so `javascript:`/`data:` would be a stored-XSS sink (2026-07-20).
11. **No customer/tenant details in this repo — ever.** LogCoreOS is public. Beta tester / hosted-customer names, server IPs, hostnames, SSH details, and account info go ONLY in the private Business repo (owner rule, 2026-07-17). Docs here may reference tenants generically ("first managed-hosting beta instance") with a pointer to the Business repo.
12. **ntfy publish is authenticated once given a public hostname** (added 2026-07-20): ntfy has no auth by default (open publish, ID-gated read only) — acceptable for LAN-only use, but not once ntfy is reachable from the internet. `launch.sh`'s `provision_ntfy_auth()` creates one admin "publisher" account + token (`NTFY_PUBLISH_TOKEN` in `docker/.env`) and sets `NTFY_AUTH_DEFAULT_ACCESS=read-only` so anonymous subscribe still needs zero setup while publish requires the app's token (sent as `Authorization: Bearer` in `notification_service.send()`). Idempotent (skipped once the token is recorded) and fails open (any provisioning step failing leaves ntfy in default read-write rather than silently breaking all notifications).
13. **`X-Workspace` is entitlement-checked in `get_current_user()`, never trust a bare `Depends(get_workspace)` value from anywhere else** (fixed 2026-08-12): the header only had to be literally `"personal"`/`"business"` to be accepted — nothing checked it against the caller's own `user["workspaces"]` grant, so a user without business access could still self-serve read/write their own `Business/` folder just by sending the header. Fixed by flipping the dependency direction: `get_current_user()` now reads the raw `X-Workspace` header directly (`Header(default="personal")`) and coerces it against `effective_ws` (the existing entitlement-intersection logic, previously computed but silently discarded for this purpose) before setting `user["_workspace"]`; `get_workspace()` now just returns `current_user["_workspace"]` via `Depends(get_current_user)`. Every router already declares `workspace: str = Depends(get_workspace)` unchanged — they all got the fix for free since FastAPI resolves that dependency through the same (now-safe) chain. **How to apply:** any time a request-scoped value needs to be validated against the authenticated user, make the dependency that provides it depend on `get_current_user` (or take the value from the resolved user object) rather than resolving it independently from raw request data — two independently-resolved dependencies over the same header/param can silently diverge.
14. **Read-modify-write on a per-user JSON store must go through `file_service.update_json()`, never a bare `read_json()` + `write_json()` pair** (fixed 2026-08-12, `task_service.py`): `write_json()`'s lock only ever covered the write step, so two concurrent writers could both read the same pre-mutation state and the second write would silently clobber the first's change. Demonstrated for real, not just theorized: 20 concurrent unlocked increments against the same counter file produced a final value of **1**, not 20. `update_json(path, mutate, default)` holds one `threading.RLock` (upgraded from a plain `Lock` so it can safely call the existing `write_json()` internally without deadlocking) across the full read+mutate+write cycle. `task_service.py`'s four write functions (`add_task`/`update_task`/`delete_task`/`cleanup_done_goals`) are migrated; every other per-user JSON store (Contacts, Assets, Finance, Dashboards, Notes, …) still uses the unlocked pattern and has the same theoretical exposure — tracked in the Idea Backlog as a follow-up migration, not yet done.
15. **Deleting a custom feature role warns with real names, not just a generic message** (fixed 2026-08-12): `GET /admin/features/roles/{role_name}/users` returns who's currently assigned a role; `RoleDefinitions.jsx`'s delete confirm calls it first and builds an informed message (e.g. "1 user currently has it — Bob Worker") instead of a blind "Users with this role will fall back to member." Deliberately a **warn**, not a **block** — the role stays deletable (TASKS.md's original item offered "warn (or block)"; block would be a bigger behavior change than asked for and `member` fallback isn't itself catastrophic, just worth knowing about before it happens).
16. **A dashboard block move/resize approval crashed the Chat page every time it rendered, including on every future reload of that conversation** (fixed 2026-08-16, real production crash): `Chat.jsx`'s `DashboardBlockPreview`, in the `update_dashboard_block` branch, declared `const layout = step.input.layout ? { ...(layout.x != null ? ...) } : b.layout` — referencing `layout` *inside the expression that initializes `layout`*, a temporal-dead-zone violation. Threw `ReferenceError: Cannot access uninitialized variable` (Safari's exact wording) deterministically, every single time a proposed layout override was actually present — i.e. every time the agent's move/resize request looked like it should render its preview card. Because the crash happened on *render*, not on click, the already-broken message was permanently saved in the chat archive, and Chat's own "reopen last conversation on load" behavior meant the app crashed on startup every time until fixed. Fixed by renaming the ternary's source value to `override` so the `const layout` being built is never self-referenced mid-initialization. **How to apply:** never reference the name being declared by a `const`/`let` from within its own initializer expression, even indirectly through a nested object/ternary — pick a different name for the source value (`override`, `input`, etc.) rather than reusing the target name. A first, unrelated crash reported in the same session (`undefined is not an object (evaluating 'e.split')`, `Markdown`'s `text.split('\n')` on an undefined message) couldn't be pinned to a specific backend condition (`final_answer` always has a non-empty fallback in every pause branch checked) but was hardened anyway — `Markdown` now defaults to an empty string, since a pure-tool-call turn with no prose text is a legitimate case that should render blank, not crash the page.

---

## Known Gotchas — mobile keyboard (resolved 2026-08-16 — attempt 12, see full investigation below)

**Any text `<input>`/`<textarea>` rendered under 16px triggers iOS Safari's auto-zoom-on-focus, with no auto-zoom-back-out (fixed for Notes/Journal, 2026-08-15)**
`Notes.jsx`'s and `Journal.jsx`'s editor `<textarea>` both used Tailwind's `text-sm` (14px) — below the 16px threshold iOS Safari uses to decide a focused field is "too small to read," so it auto-zooms the whole page in on focus and never zooms back out on its own (reported: "hides the edges and looks bad, have to manually zoom back out"). Fixed by bumping both to `text-base` (16px) — the standard, well-established threshold, not a visual preference. The app's shared `.input` class doesn't set an explicit `font-size` at all (inherits the 16px root default), so ordinary form fields elsewhere were never affected — this was isolated to these two editors' own explicit override.
**How to apply:** Never set a text-entry field's rendered font-size below 16px on a page reachable on mobile — check any future `text-sm`/`text-xs` on an `<input>`/`<textarea>`/`contentEditable` element specifically (labels, buttons, and read-only text are unaffected).

**Chat composer not rising above the on-screen keyboard — history of four attempts; real-device feedback on the third revealed iOS doesn't pick one consistent keyboard strategy (2026-08-15)**
Attempts 1 (`visualViewport`-based JS scroll correction) and 2 (`sticky bottom-0` CSS, reverted after it broke Chat's own layout and spread the identical symptom into Notes) both assumed the *page itself* would resize correctly in response to the keyboard, via `interactive-widget=resizes-content` cascading through `100%`/`dvh` sizing. A deeper investigation found real reasons to doubt that (a deliberate `html`/`body` oversizing hack for an unrelated bug, plus Safari's `interactive-widget` support only starting at 17.4). Attempt 3 dropped that assumption and instead computed `window.innerHeight - (visualViewport.height + .offsetTop)` — the gap between what the layout viewport claims is visible and what's actually visible — moving only the composer by that amount.
**Real-device feedback on attempt 3 (owner, same day): "most of the time it didn't do anything... sometimes it will move only that box up and it will be way above the keyboard... other times it will move the whole page up, footer and all."** This showed `window.innerHeight` itself is not a reliable reference on this device — iOS doesn't consistently pick one strategy (pure resize, pure pan, or a native scroll-into-view that partially handles it already), so a formula anchored to `window.innerHeight` produces a different, sometimes-wrong answer depending on which strategy happened that time: correct when nothing needed to move, an overshoot when only *some* of the composer was actually hidden (the formula assumed the *entire* keyboard height needed compensating, not just the actual overlap), and double-compensation when the browser's own native pan already moved the whole page (composer's correction stacking on top of a shift it didn't know had already happened).
**Attempt 4 drops `window.innerHeight` entirely.** Instead it measures the composer's own actual on-screen position via `getBoundingClientRect()` — which is layout-viewport-relative and does *not* move just because iOS pans the visual viewport — and compares it against the visual viewport's current bottom edge (`visualViewport.offsetTop + .height`, also layout-viewport-relative). The gap between those two is exactly how much of the composer is *actually* hidden right now, regardless of *why* (resize, pan, or a native scroll that already did some of the work). Whatever transform is already applied is added back onto the measured `rect.bottom` before comparing, since `getBoundingClientRect()` reflects the post-transform position — this is what prevents double-compensating on top of the page's own native panning: if the browser already revealed the composer, the computed gap is correctly ~0.
**Verified**: the exact `reposition()` formula, unit-tested in isolation (not through a live DOM) against four numeric scenarios modeling all three reported failure modes plus a clean retract-on-keyboard-close case — each produces the mathematically correct result (0 when already visible, the *exact* overlap rather than a flat overshoot when partially covered, 0 when a native pan already revealed it, 0 on retract). Also re-confirmed the mechanical mount check (effect mounts with no console errors, `composerRef` resolves to the one real composer element). **What remains unverifiable from here, same as every attempt before it**: Chromium has no real on-screen keyboard and can't reproduce iOS's actual inconsistent resize/pan/native-scroll behavior, so the live event-driven trigger path itself still needs the owner's own device to confirm.
**How to apply:** A "gap" calculation for viewport/keyboard-avoidance code should be anchored to two *directly comparable, same-coordinate-space* measurements of the element and the visible area (`getBoundingClientRect()` + `visualViewport`'s own offset/size) — never to `window.innerHeight`, which several attempts' worth of real-device feedback now shows is not a reliable "what should be visible" reference on real mobile Safari; it can lag, stay stale during a pure pan, or simply not reflect what the browser actually did.
**Attempt 5 (owner's own idea, same day): move the whole page as one unit instead of the composer alone — first attempt confirmed *mostly* working on a real device.** Attempt 4's math was verified correct, but real-device feedback was still "still broken" — most likely because an isolated floating composer (correctly positioned on its own, but detached from the messages list above it, which stayed put) is itself a visually broken result even with technically-correct math: a gap or overlap where the two used to sit flush together. Attempt 5 keeps attempt 4's proven-correct gap formula (still measured from the composer's own position — it's still the element that must clear the keyboard) but applies the resulting transform to the whole page's own top-level container (new `pageRef`) instead of the composer alone (`composerRef` is now measurement-only), so the header/messages/composer all move together and nothing floats independently. The bottom tab bar needs no special handling — `Layout.jsx` renders it outside this page entirely, so shifting Chat's own container never moves it; the keyboard just covers whatever's underneath once it's tall enough, tab bar included — which is exactly the owner's own framing of the fix ("keyboard covers the footer but not the chat box").
**A real regression caught before shipping, same class as the image-lightbox bug**: the Chats drawer (`fixed inset-0 z-50`) was a descendant of the same container `pageRef`'s transform now applies to — `transform` creates a new containing block for `position: fixed` descendants (see the lightbox entry above), so the drawer would have rendered relative to `pageRef`'s shifted position instead of the true viewport once a keyboard-driven shift was active. Fixed by restructuring the component's return to a `<>` fragment with the drawer as a **sibling** of the `pageRef` div, not a descendant — verified mechanically (opened the drawer, applied a manual shift to `pageRef`, confirmed the drawer's `getBoundingClientRect()` still exactly matches the true viewport regardless).
**Owner's live real-device report on attempt 5: "it seems to work most of the time."** Real, meaningful progress — genuinely different from attempts 1-4, which were each reported as fully broken. The owner's own follow-up suspicion for the remaining rough edge: the messages list's own scroll interacting with the keyboard-driven reposition (`visualViewport` fires both `resize` and `scroll` events; a user scroll gesture on the messages list while the keyboard is up could plausibly trigger a spurious recompute mid-gesture). Added `keyboardOpen` state (set from the same `reposition()` call, `true` whenever a shift > 1px is currently applied) that swaps the messages container from `overflow-y-auto` to `overflow-hidden` while the keyboard is up — `overflow-hidden` only disables scroll input, it doesn't reset `scrollTop`, so the prior scroll position is exactly preserved once `overflow-y-auto` returns on keyboard close.
**How to apply (position vs. transform-target lesson):** When a "move this element to avoid X" fix computes a *correct* offset but the visible result still looks wrong, check whether the fix is being applied to the right *scope* — an element positioned correctly in isolation can still look broken if it's now visually detached from siblings that used to move with it. And whenever adding a `transform` to any container, audit its descendants for `position: fixed` before shipping — this is the second time this exact class of bug has been caught in one session (see the image-lightbox entry above), and it's easy to introduce silently since nothing errors, it just silently mispositions.

**Attempt 6 (same day): all JS transform code removed entirely, reverted to relying on native `interactive-widget=resizes-content` alone.** Owner's real-device screenshot of attempt 5 + the scroll-lock showed a large, unwanted blank gap between the composer and the keyboard, plus "glitches and shoots the screen everywhere and gets jittery" on scroll — worse than attempt 5's earlier "works most of the time" report, not better. Root-cause theory (not directly instrumented on-device, reasoned from the evidence): `index.html`'s viewport meta already declares `interactive-widget=resizes-content`, which on supporting Safari (17.4+) shrinks the *layout* viewport itself — meaning this page's own flex layout already reflows above the keyboard with zero JS. Attempt 5's `reposition()` was computing and applying an *additional* shift on top of that native resize, double-counting the same correction — which matches the observed overshoot (dead space where the native resize already made room) exactly. The `scroll`-driven jitter is consistent with the same double-correction compounding mid-gesture as `visualViewport`'s `scroll` event fires during ordinary scrolling, not just keyboard transitions.
**What changed**: `Chat.jsx`'s entire `reposition()` effect, the `keyboardOpen` state and its `overflow-hidden` scroll-lock, and the `pageRef`/`composerRef` transform wiring were all removed — the composer is back to plain flow with no JS positioning. The Chats-drawer sibling restructuring (fixing the separate `transform`-containing-block trap) was kept since it's a real correctness fix independent of whether a transform is currently applied. This makes attempt 6 an isolation step as much as a fix: if the composer is still hidden behind the keyboard on the owner's device, that tells us `interactive-widget=resizes-content` isn't taking effect there (old Safari, or something else overriding it) — useful, clean information attempt 5 couldn't produce since its own JS was masking whatever the native behavior actually does. **Not yet verified on a real device — the owner's own test is the next step.**
**How to apply:** Before adding JS-computed viewport-correction code, check whether a CSS-level fix (here, a meta tag already present) is already supposed to be handling it — layering a second, independently-computed correction on top of a working native mechanism produces exactly this failure shape (overshoot + jitter), and unlike a missing fix, it's a regression the base browser behavior alone wouldn't have had.

**Attempt 7 (owner's idea, same day): scroll the composer into view instead of computing and applying our own offset.** Every prior JS attempt (3-6) worked by measuring a gap and applying it as a `transform` — a value we captured once and could get stale, wrong, or stacked on top of a native correction we didn't know had already happened (exactly what broke attempt 5). Scrolling is a different mechanism entirely: `composerRef.current.scrollIntoView({ block: 'end' })`, called on every `visualViewport` `resize` event, asks the *browser* to reconcile the element's position against whatever the current visual viewport actually is, live, at the moment it runs — nothing for our own code to get out of sync. `<main>` (`Layout.jsx`) is the real scrollable ancestor the composer lives inside (`overflow-y-scroll`); scrolling it so the composer's bottom edge sits flush with the bottom of the visible area is exactly "scroll to the bottom" for this layout. Idempotent on an already-in-view element, so firing it on keyboard-close too is harmless. Far smaller and simpler than any attempt since 3 — no state, no transform, no scroll-lock.
**Real-device feedback: worked on first open, but closing and reopening the keyboard within ~10 seconds stopped it from working — a "cooldown" symptom — plus a separately-reported "the entire page glitches down when the keyboard opens."** Both traced to the initial `behavior: 'smooth'` option. WebKit has a known quirk where a `smooth` scroll on a container, interrupted by another scroll command before it finishes animating, can leave that container refusing new scroll commands for several seconds until the stale animation resolves — matching the cooldown exactly. An animated scroll racing the browser's own native scroll-into-view-on-focus behavior is also exactly what a visible glitch/double-jump would look like. Switched to `behavior: 'instant'` — no animation, nothing to get interrupted or race against.
**Instant scroll changed neither symptom** (real-device follow-up, same day: "the original fix works but it still only works every few seconds" / "its still double jumping the page") — ruling out the animation as the cause. Reasoned conclusion: Safari already auto-scrolls a focused input into view natively on its own; our resize-triggered `scrollIntoView` was very likely firing as a *second*, separate scroll shortly after Safari's own — exactly what a double jump looks like — regardless of whether that second scroll animates or snaps instantly. And `visualViewport`'s `resize` event is a documented-flaky trigger on iOS, not guaranteed to fire on every keyboard transition — consistent with "only works every few seconds."

**Attempt 8: removed the corrective scroll entirely — zero JS, matching attempt 6.** A gap in the process worth naming: the owner proposed attempt 7 ("what about just scrolling to the bottom") immediately after attempt 6 shipped, before any real-device feedback on attempt 6 alone came back — so the true zero-JS baseline (native `interactive-widget=resizes-content` resize plus Safari's own native focus-scroll, no correction at all) was never actually isolated and confirmed on its own. Every JS attempt since 7 has had *some* correction layered on top influencing the result, so "does native alone need help at all" is still an open, unanswered question. Attempt 8 answers it cleanly: `Chat.jsx` now has no keyboard-avoidance code whatsoever.
**Real-device result: the double jump and periodic auto-scroll-down were STILL there with zero JS in Chat.jsx** — proving none of attempts 3-8 (all scoped to `Chat.jsx`) were ever actually the cause. This reframed the whole investigation: the bug was never about Chat's composer positioning specifically.

**The actual root cause, found by comparison rather than another guess.** Asked the owner a scoping question instead of shipping a ninth Chat-only change: does the same jump/scroll happen on other pages? Answer: **"happens everywhere that has scroll enabled. only notes and journal dont have issue cause they have scroll locked."** This is the finding that actually cracked it — it's not Chat-specific at all, and it's not about composer position: it's `<main>` (`Layout.jsx`, shared by every page, `overflow-y-scroll`) being an actual scrollable container. WebKit's native "keep the focused field visible" correction acts on whichever scrollable ancestor a focused field sits inside; combined with `interactive-widget=resizes-content` reflowing the layout at the same moment, that correction visibly runs in separate passes (the "double jump") and can re-trigger more than once (the "periodic" scroll). Pages whose content never overflows `<main>` (Notes, Journal) give WebKit nothing to correct, so they never showed the bug — not because of anything special in their own code, purely because they don't scroll.
**Attempt 9: lock `<main>`'s own scroll while a text field inside it is focused, in `Layout.jsx` (app-wide, not Chat.jsx).** First cut just toggled `mainRef.current.style.overflowY` between `'hidden'` and `''` on `focusin`/`focusout` (`relatedTarget` checked so field-to-field taps stay locked instead of flickering). Scoped to `(pointer: coarse)` (touch) devices only — no on-screen keyboard to correct for on desktop, and locking scroll there would break the ordinary desktop habit of mouse-wheel-scrolling a long form while a field still has focus.
**Real-device result: "nothing changed" — no improvement at all.** `overflow: hidden` is a well-documented unreliable way to stop scrolling on iOS Safari specifically; WebKit's internal keyboard-driven "scroll the focused field into view" behavior is a lower-level UA mechanism that doesn't reliably respect it the way an ordinary user touch-drag does.
**Attempt 9b: same idea, more robust mechanism — actively snap `<main>`'s `scrollTop` back instead of just setting `overflow: hidden`.** On focus of a field, captures `main.scrollTop` and attaches a `scroll` listener that resets it back to that captured value the instant it changes, for *any* reason — including WebKit's own internal correction, not just user-driven scroll. Deliberately **not** `position: fixed` (the other, more-robust-than-`overflow:hidden` iOS scroll-lock trick): freezing `<main>`'s *size* that way would also stop it from *shrinking* for the keyboard, which is exactly what `interactive-widget=resizes-content` needs to do to keep the composer visible — that would trade this bug for the original composer-hidden-behind-keyboard one back again. Only the scroll *position* needs to stay put; the size still needs to keep resizing normally.
**Verified mechanically** (standalone Playwright checks against the exact logic, not the live app): confirmed a forced `scrollTop` change while locked gets actively snapped back to the position captured at focus time — not just passively prevented — and confirmed it releases correctly once focus leaves the field.
**Real-device result: "nothing changed again.." — zero effect, a second time.** Two structurally different scroll-position-locking techniques both doing nothing is itself the diagnostic signal: the visible motion is very likely not a `scrollTop` change at all.
