**Attempt 10: a different variable entirely — smooth `<main>`'s height reflow with CSS, not another scroll-position fix.** `html`/`body`/`#root` all resolve their height against the layout viewport (see `index.css`'s phantom-toolbar comment — the same oversizing hack for a different iOS PWA quirk, confirming this chain is real and already known to matter here). `interactive-widget=resizes-content` shrinks that layout viewport progressively across the keyboard's own multi-frame opening animation (typically ~250-300ms), not instantly — cascading straight into `<main>`'s `flex-1 min-h-0` height. Ordinary layout reflow is not interpolated by the browser the way `transform`/`opacity`/explicitly-transitioned properties are, so each intermediate frame of that resize renders as a discrete jump. Separately, browsers auto-clamp a scrollable container's `scrollTop` whenever its height shrinks below the current scroll position — which would fire on *every* one of those reflow frames, independent of any WebKit focus-scroll behavior, giving a clean mechanistic explanation for "periodic auto-scroll down" that has nothing to do with keyboard-focus correction at all. Both scroll-lock attempts (9, 9b) were defending the wrong variable — neither one stops `<main>`'s *height* from changing, and 9b's snap-back was fighting the browser's own per-frame clamp reactively rather than addressing the underlying reflow jank.
Removed both scroll-lock attempts from `Layout.jsx` entirely (the now-unused `mainRef` and its focus-tracking effect). Added `transition-[height] duration-300 ease-out` directly to `<main>`'s className — a pure CSS change, no JS, so each intermediate reflow frame during the keyboard's animation interpolates smoothly instead of jumping straight to the new value. `duration-300` roughly matches iOS's typical keyboard-animation timing.
**Real-device result: "nothing changed" — a third real-device round with zero measurable effect.** Confirmed via a direct deploy-check that the build genuinely reached the device both times. Three structurally different fixes (JS transform/scroll, two scroll-locks, a CSS reflow transition) all doing literally nothing is itself the strongest signal yet: not "the theory was imprecise," but "the theory is very likely wrong about what's actually visible on screen."

**Owner ask: "brainstorm what other things it could be maybe higher up than youve been looking."** Two things followed from taking that literally: (1) confirmed the bug happens in a plain mobile browser tab too, not just the installed PWA — ruling out a standalone-display-mode-specific WebKit quirk (there's a *proven* precedent for exactly that split in this app, for the unrelated phantom-toolbar footer-nav bug, which is why this was worth checking directly rather than assuming). (2) Re-examined `Layout.jsx`'s **pre-existing** keyboard touch-offset effect (from *before* this whole investigation, added for a different bug) rather than more code from the last 10 attempts.

**Attempt 11: found and fixed a real bug in that pre-existing effect.** Its `onViewportResize` fires a `window.scrollTo(0,0)` correction whenever `Math.abs(vv.height - window.innerHeight) < 1`, on the documented assumption that only happens once the keyboard is fully closed — true under the *older* pan-based keyboard behavior, where `window.innerHeight` stays constant while only the visual viewport pans. It does **not** hold under `interactive-widget=resizes-content` (what this app actually uses): that mode's entire purpose is keeping the layout viewport (`window.innerHeight`) shrinking in lockstep with the visual viewport, so the two can stay near-equal on *every* resize tick throughout the keyboard's whole opening/closing animation, not just at the end — meaning this correction could have been firing a forced `scrollTo(0,0)` many times per keyboard transition instead of once. Debounced it: `onViewportResize` now clears/resets a 150ms timer on every tick and only checks-and-corrects once resize events have genuinely stopped, instead of on every qualifying tick.
**Real-device result: "nothing changed" — a fourth real-device round with zero effect, in four genuinely different technical directions (JS transform/scroll, two scroll-locks, a CSS reflow transition, a debounce fix in pre-existing code).** At this point, continuing to write new fixes blind wasn't a good use of further rebuild cycles, and was said so directly rather than proposing a fifth. The owner has no Mac for Safari Web Inspector, so real diagnostics needed a different path.

**Added an on-device debug console instead of another guess** (`app/frontend/src/main.jsx`) — `eruda`, dynamically imported and gated behind `?debug=1` (never loaded on a normal page view), bundled as an npm dependency rather than a CDN script so it works under the app's existing CSP with no CSP change. See `docs/AGENTS.md`'s Development Setup section for how to use it. This isn't a fix — it's a way to stop guessing and start seeing real computed styles/console output/layout info on the actual device, which every one of the last 11 attempts was missing.
**Owner report: the debug console "doesn't work how it should" — removed, and asked to reset everything to zero.** No further detail was given on what specifically went wrong with eruda (the request was to remove it, not diagnose it), so no root cause is recorded here. All of it — `eruda` (`main.jsx`, `package.json`), the CSS `transition-[height]` on `<main>` (attempt 10), and the `onViewportResize` debounce (attempt 11) — was removed, restoring `Layout.jsx`/`Chat.jsx` to their exact state from *before* this investigation began (verified via `git diff`, hunk by hunk, against the last commit). The one thing deliberately **not** reverted: the original Round B touch-offset fix itself (the `useEffect` this all lives inside) — that's a separate, already-confirmed bug fix predating the double-jump investigation entirely, not one of the 11 attempts.
**State as of this reset: the double-jump/periodic-auto-scroll-down bug is unresolved and unfixed, no code changes in flight for it.** Eleven attempts across four genuinely different technical layers (JS transform/scroll, two scroll-locks, a CSS reflow transition, a debounce fix, plus an aborted diagnostic-tooling attempt) — a real, confirmed pattern of no measurable progress worth being honest about before starting a twelfth.

**Attempt 12 (2026-08-16) — went back through git history instead of guessing forward, per owner request ("check previous commits for the one that didn't have this keyboard issue... i know it used to not have this bug").** `git log -- app/frontend/index.html app/frontend/src/components/Layout.jsx` showed both `interactive-widget=resizes-content` (`index.html`'s viewport meta) and `Layout.jsx`'s entire keyboard touch-offset effect were **absent from every single commit in this repo's history** until the giant catch-up commit made earlier this same session — the last real commit before that (`9187e80`, from weeks prior) had a plain viewport meta with no `interactive-widget` directive at all, and zero keyboard-handling code in `Layout.jsx`. Neither file was touched by any of the ~149 commits in between. This is hard evidence, not a guess: the entire keyboard-handling apparatus this whole investigation has been fighting was added recently, in the same span of uncommitted work this investigation itself happened in.
**Working theory, now well-supported rather than speculative:** `interactive-widget=resizes-content` was added specifically to fix the *original* "composer doesn't rise above the keyboard" complaint (predating this whole double-jump saga). It's plausible that single change destabilized keyboard behavior in more than one way — both the touch-offset desync bug (patched by the effect this section describes) *and* the double-jump/periodic-scroll bug (never successfully patched across 11 attempts) could be downstream side effects of the same root decision, with every fix since chasing a symptom rather than the actual cause.
**Reverted both all the way back to the true pre-existing baseline**: `index.html`'s viewport meta is back to `width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0, viewport-fit=cover` (no `interactive-widget`), and `Layout.jsx`'s entire touch-offset `useEffect` (the `visualViewport`/`focusin` correction) is removed entirely — not just the debounce tweak from attempt 11, the whole thing. Verified via `git diff` against the old baseline commit that both files now match exactly on these points.
**Known, accepted tradeoff**: the original "composer hidden behind keyboard" complaint that motivated adding `interactive-widget=resizes-content` may resurface, since its fix is now gone too. Owner's explicit call: deal with that as a fresh, separate problem if it comes back, rather than keep building on a foundation that's produced 11 failed attempts and at least one other bug (touch-offset) as apparent side effects.
**Confirmed fixed on the real device (2026-08-16): "i tested it. it all works as it should."** After 11 failed forward-guessing attempts, going back through git history to find and restore a state that provably existed and worked, rather than layering another fix on an already-unstable foundation, is what actually resolved this. The composer-hidden-behind-keyboard bug this was traded for did not resurface in testing — no follow-up work needed for now. **How to apply, generally:** when a long chain of targeted fixes keeps failing against the same symptom, check whether the underlying mechanism being patched around is itself recent and optional (via `git log` on the specific files/lines involved) before adding a twelfth patch — reverting a whole recent approach can succeed where refining it repeatedly does not, especially for newer/less-mature platform features (`interactive-widget=resizes-content` here, Safari 17.4+ and evidently still rough).

## Environment Variables (config.py)

All env vars are defined in `app/backend/config.py` via Pydantic Settings and read from `docker/.env`.

| Variable | Default | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `BRAIN_PATH` | `/data/brain` | Volume mount path for Brain files |
| `SECRET_KEY` | `change-me-in-production` | JWT signing key — **must be changed** |
| `AI_PROVIDER` | `anthropic` | `anthropic` or `openai` (OpenAI-compatible) |
| `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` | `""` | Set via env or Admin UI (stored in `brain/ai_settings.json`) |
| `AI_MODEL` | `claude-sonnet-4-6` | Model identifier |
| `AI_API_KEY` | `""` | For non-Anthropic providers |
| `AI_BASE_URL` | `""` | Custom endpoint; empty = provider default |
| `TAVILY_API_KEY` | `""` | Web search in chat research mode |
| `NTFY_URL` | `http://ntfy:80` | Internal ntfy notification endpoint |
| `WHATS_NEW_DAYS` | `5` | Days the What's-New banner stays visible after an app update |
| `COOKIE_SECURE` | `True` | Set `False` for local HTTP dev |
| `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` | `*` | Comma-separated allowed CORS origins |
| `SCHEDULER_TIMEZONE` | `America/Chicago` | IANA timezone for all scheduler jobs |
| `MORNING_DIGEST_HOUR` | `6` | Hour (0–23) for daily digest job |
| `OVERDUE_CHECK_HOUR` | `19` | Hour (0–23) for overdue + weekly + goal drift jobs |
| `ALLOW_OPEN_REGISTRATION` | `False` | `True` = open signup (dev/demo only) |
| `TRUST_PROXY_HEADERS` | `False` | `True` when behind nginx/Caddy |

## Known Gotchas

**A bare-element CSS selector in a per-user theme override can silently outrank a Tailwind arbitrary-value utility on the exact same element, and only manifests for users with that theme setting on (found 2026-08-26, real owner report — "chat box floating well above the footer... that fix corrected the issue on every other instance but broke my personal one"):** The owner correctly remembered the 2026-08-22 fix (below) that removed Chat's duplicate mobile-nav-clearance spacer, and reasonably assumed its removal had regressed. It hadn't — confirmed by diffing the current `Chat.jsx` against the pre-fix committed version (only import paths differed) and by rendering the real bottom-nav markup with the owner's actual pinned shortcuts (`Dashboard`/`Tasks`/`AI Chat`/`Household`, from `auth.json`) at every mobile width from 320px to 430px: it never wraps, so that wasn't it either — a real dead end, reported to the owner as such rather than quietly abandoned. The owner then supplied the decisive fact: the same instance/build was broken on their phone while *other* instances, opened on that *same* phone, rendered correctly — ruling out the device/browser and stale-cache theories in one message, since a caching or browser-quirk explanation can't produce different results for the same client hitting genuinely identical markup. That reframed it as a per-account difference, not a per-device one. `brain/_system/auth.json` showed the owner's personal-instance account has `density: "compact"` (the only account on that instance with it set). `index.css`'s compact override was `html.compact main { @apply p-3 md:p-4; }` — a *shorthand* `padding` declaration, and `html.compact main`'s selector specificity (element + class + element = 0,0,1,2) beats a single Tailwind utility class like `.pb-\[calc\(4.5rem+env\(safe-area-inset-bottom\)\)\]` (0,0,1,0) regardless of source order, so the shorthand's `padding-bottom: 0.75rem` (12px) silently won over the intended ~72px+safe-area nav clearance — collapsing it to a sixth of what the fixed 53px nav bar needs, for every compact-density user, on every page, not just Chat (Chat's bottom-anchored composer just made it visible first, the same "only reported where content hugs the edge" pattern as the 2026-08-22 entry below). **Fix**: scoped the compact override to `pt-3 px-3 md:pt-4 md:px-4` — top/left/right only, never touching `padding-bottom`, so `<main>`'s own nav-clearance utility (or `md:p-6`'s desktop bottom padding) is never shadowed regardless of density setting. **How to apply:** a bare-element selector (`main`, `h1`, etc.) combined with even one class is easy to under-estimate on specificity — it can beat a Tailwind utility class outright, and a shorthand property (`padding`, `margin`, `border`) silently overrides every longhand sub-property another rule set individually, even ones that rule never intended to touch. Any future per-theme/per-density CSS override on a shared layout element should target only the specific sides/properties it means to change, never a shorthand, precisely to avoid clobbering an unrelated longhand override elsewhere in the cascade. **Verified**: measured `getComputedStyle(main).paddingBottom` under `html.compact` directly in a headless browser against the real compiled CSS — 12px before the fix (confirmed via the `@apply p-3` source), 72px after, matching `<main>`'s own intended value exactly; `eslint`/`vite build` clean.

**Mobile bottom-nav clearance was reserved twice on the Chat page — once in the shared page shell, once again per-page — and the duplicate is far more visible on a page with a bottom-anchored composer than on a scrolling list (2026-08-22):** `Layout.jsx`'s `<main>` already reserves `pb-[calc(4.5rem+env(safe-area-inset-bottom))]` (72px, comfortably more than the 53px-tall fixed mobile nav) so nothing on any page ends up hidden behind it. `Chat.jsx` additionally rendered its own `<div className="h-20 md:hidden shrink-0" />` (80px) for the identical stated purpose ("Clears the fixed mobile footer nav so the input row is never hidden behind it") — a second, independent reservation stacked on top of the first, ~152px total to clear a 53px bar. The same exact spacer, same comment wording, is copy-pasted across `Journal.jsx`, `Tasks.jsx`, `Calendar.jsx`, `Contacts.jsx`, `Household.jsx`, and `Brain.jsx` too — genuinely redundant everywhere it appears, but only reported as a bug on Chat, because trailing whitespace after the last item in a scrolling list reads as normal while a composer that's supposed to hug the bottom visibly floating does not. **Fix (this round, deliberately scoped to Chat only — the owner asked for just the reported page, not a sweep)**: removed Chat's own spacer; `<main>`'s existing padding is suffient on its own. The identical redundancy still exists on the other six pages, unaddressed by choice — pull into a real task if it's ever reported there too, rather than assuming it's already covered by this fix. **How to apply:** when a shared layout shell already reserves space for a fixed overlay (nav bar, banner, etc.), a per-page "just in case" duplicate of that same reservation is easy to add without noticing the shell already handles it — check the shell's own padding/clearance before adding a page-local one for the same stated purpose. **Verified**: reproduced on a byte-identical isolated build at a real mobile viewport (iPhone 13 emulation) — composer-to-nav-bar gap measured 108px before the fix, 28px after (exactly the removed spacer's height), confirmed visually with a highlighted screenshot. See the entry directly below for why "the owner's personal instance never showed this" turned out to be a red herring, not a real signal.

**This dev machine's "personal instance" bind-mounts this exact repo checkout — any local frontend rebuild here takes effect on it immediately, live, making it useless as an independent comparison point (2026-08-22):** While chasing the report above, confirmed via `/proc/<pid>/mountinfo` that the personal instance's long-running container (`uvicorn main:app --port 8000`, up since 2026-08-18) has `/frontend/dist` and `/data/brain` bind-mounted straight from `/home/logcore/LogCoreDEV/LogCoreOS/app/frontend/dist` and `.../brain` — this exact working directory. Every `npm run build` run in this repo during a debugging session (there were several, for unrelated earlier fixes) silently redeployed to the personal instance in real time, no restart or update step needed — so "it's fine on personal" only ever meant "it's fine as of whichever local rebuild most recently ran," not "the shipped release is fine." The two VPS instances only receive a fix once it's released and their own `update.sh` pulls it, so they kept showing the bug long after local testing said it was resolved. **How to apply:** never treat this machine's personal instance as an independent, pre-fix baseline when comparing against a remote instance for a *frontend* bug — it is, for all practical purposes, a live preview of whatever this repo's `app/frontend/dist` currently contains, not a separately-deployed release. A real comparison needs either a genuinely separate checkout/container, or reasoning from the actual deployed commit + a from-scratch isolated reproduction, not "does the personal instance still show it."

**A hard startup refusal keyed on a static pre-boot value can strand an already-deployed instance on the wrong side of its own fix, permanently (2026-08-22):** The 2026-08-14 CORS hardening commit made `main.py::_startup_checks()` `sys.exit(1)` on a wildcard `ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, with a documented self-heal (`migrate_insecure_cors()`, added to both `launch.sh` and `update.sh`) that auto-derives a real origin from `brain/hosting.json`'s `domain_url` when one exists. Two real deployed instances (the public demo, a managed-hosting client) hit this the moment they tried to update past the hardening commit — new container crashes at startup, health check fails, `update.sh` rolls the *entire working tree, including update.sh itself* back to the last-good commit. That commit predates the self-heal function, so the very code that would have fixed it doesn't exist yet on disk to run — every subsequent cron tick repeats the identical failure forever, silently (the app stays up on the old version throughout; nothing looks broken from outside). Confirmed via two independent instances rolling back to the identical commit at nearly the identical timestamp. **Fix**: moved the check itself into `main.py` — before refusing anything, it now calls the same `hosting_service.effective_domain_url()` the CORS middleware already trusts at request time; a configured real domain makes the static env var moot (the middleware never even reads it in that branch), and when *nothing* is configured yet, it falls back to a safe localhost-only default instead of refusing to boot at all, rather than bricking a fresh instance before its owner can even reach Admin → Hosting to configure a domain in the first place. **How to apply:** a shell-script-level self-heal for a Python-level hard-fail is fragile by construction — the shell script only gets replaced *after* a successful update, but the failure it's supposed to prevent happens *during* the attempt, using whatever shell script is already on disk. Any new hard `sys.exit()`-style startup gate needs its escape hatch living in the same commit, in the same layer (the app code that gets rebuilt on every attempt, not the orchestration script that only updates on success) — and should prefer consulting a live, already-correct runtime config source over a static env var wherever one exists, so a future stricter check can't strand itself the same way. **Verified**: reproduced all three branches directly (domain configured → uses it, unchanged env var; nothing configured → falls back to localhost, no exit; `ALLOW_INSECURE_CORS=true` → unchanged loud-warning behavior) against an isolated fixture; full suite green.

**A workspace-scoped folder nested *inside* the base it's scoped from needs an explicit exclusion everywhere that base gets walked or path-resolved — otherwise recursive listing leaks across the boundary, and path traversal can bypass it outright (found live, 2026-08-22, via a real "Failed to load that chat" report):** `ws_path(name, "business")` is `brain/USERS/{name}/Business/` — a literal subfolder of `ws_path(name, "personal")` (`brain/USERS/{name}/`), not a sibling directory. `routers/brain.py` (`GET/PUT /brain/files/{path}`, used by `Brain.jsx` and by `Chat.jsx`'s `openSession()` to reload a saved conversation) never accepted a `workspace` parameter at all — every request resolved against `user_path(name)` (personal only) regardless of the `X-Workspace` header the frontend was already sending on every call. Any chat conversation held in the business workspace writes its archive to `Business/Chats/{filename}.md`; reopening it always looked in `Chats/{filename}.md` instead and 404'd — reported as "it errored, and now it says Failed to load that chat," which reads like corruption but is really just "the file exists, at a path the app never looked." **A second, more serious bug surfaced while fixing the first**: making `brain.py` workspace-aware by switching to `ws_path(name, workspace)` alone wasn't enough — since `Business/` is physically nested under the personal base, a plain recursive listing of "personal" walked straight into it and surfaced business files as if they were personal ones, and worse, a personal-workspace request for the literal path `Business/<file>.md` would *resolve successfully* (it's a real file, genuinely inside the personal base's directory tree) — a real cross-workspace read/write bypass, not just a display bug. **Fix**: added `"Business"` to the router's existing `_ALLOWED_DIRS_SKIP` set (previously just `{"Tasks"}`, for an unrelated reason — managed by another module) — the same mechanism already used to keep one reserved folder name out of both the recursive listing and path resolution now closes both problems in one change, since `_resolve()` and `_list_md()` both already consult that set. **How to apply:** any time a workspace/tenant/scope boundary is implemented as "the same base path, plus a reserved subfolder name" rather than a fully separate root, treat that subfolder name as a hard exclusion everywhere the *other* scope's base gets walked or resolved against — a directory listing and a path-traversal check are two different code paths that both need the same exclusion, and testing only the listing (or only a single successful-read case) won't surface the other. **Verified**: 7 new tests in `tests/test_brain.py` (previously zero coverage on this router) — business-workspace read/write succeeds, is invisible from personal, personal listing never includes a business file, and a personal-workspace request for `Business/<file>.md` is rejected with 403 rather than silently succeeding. Full suite 781 green (774 prior + 7 new).

**`INFISICAL_CACHE_KEY` is a data key** — never change it on an instance with encrypted `infisical_cache.json`/`infisical_config.json` on disk (they become permanently undecryptable, same category as `N8N_ENCRYPTION_KEY` above); `launch.sh` only generates it for fresh installs, `update.sh`'s `migrate_infisical_cache_key()` provisions it for existing ones that don't have it yet. Deliberately lives in `docker/.env`, not `brain/_system/`, since that whole directory is swept into one tarball by `backup.sh` — a key stored next to its own ciphertext would defeat the point (2026-08-22, closing out the accepted-risk CodeQL finding on `infisical_loader.py`'s plaintext secrets cache). If it isn't set, both files fall back to plaintext with a one-time boot warning rather than a hard failure — only managed-hosting instances with `INFISICAL_TOKEN` configured are exposed at all; self-hosted instances never create these files.

**A security audit's "verified sound" claims are only true as of the date they were written — a later, unrelated feature can quietly invalidate one without anyone noticing (2026-08-22):** the 2026-07-19 audit's "no stored-XSS content path" claim rested on a specific, checkable premise: "the frontend has no markdown-to-HTML renderer... because it never needs one." That premise held until `Chat.jsx` shipped its own hand-rolled markdown renderer on 2026-08-10 — a second content-to-HTML path the audit's own reasoning explicitly said didn't exist. Nobody re-checked the audit's invariant against the new feature; it sat quietly wrong for six weeks until CodeQL's automated scanning caught the same bug class in it that the audit's own A3 finding had already covered elsewhere (the automation-inbox link). **How to apply:** when an audit or security doc states an invariant in the form "X is safe because Y never happens," any future feature that makes Y happen needs to be checked against that invariant *at the time it ships*, not just left for the next full audit or scanner pass to eventually catch — the flagged design-notes rule in `SECURITY.md` ("any new feature that renders a URL as a clickable link must validate its scheme first") exists precisely to make this one recur-proof, but the general pattern (a stated "why this can't happen" premise silently stops holding) is worth watching for in any future audit-derived claim, not just this specific one.

**`scrollIntoView()` with default alignment walks every scrollable ancestor, including ones that were never meant to move — found live in production, 2026-08-22, on the two instances that just came back from the update-deadlock above:** `Chat.jsx`'s auto-scroll-to-latest-message (`bottomRef.current?.scrollIntoView({ behavior: 'smooth' })`, two call sites — on every `messages` change, and after loading a saved session) never set `block`, so it defaulted to `'start'`. Per spec, `scrollIntoView` walks *every* scrollable ancestor between the target and the document root and aligns the target against each one — not just the nearest. The sidebar's own module list (`<nav>` in `Layout.jsx`) has no scroll of its own and no height cap, so once it has enough enabled modules plus the 2026-08-18 "Pinned" section (true even for a brand-new account, since `DEFAULT_SHORTCUTS` pins three items out of the box), its content genuinely overflows the outer app-shell row (`.flex.h-full.overflow-hidden`, the sidebar+content wrapper) — which still counts as a legitimate scroll container to the browser purely because it has `overflow: hidden` (any non-`visible` overflow value creates one, whether or not anything makes it user-scrollable). Every chat auto-scroll then nudged that outer shell's `scrollTop` too, shifting the *entire app shell* (sidebar included) upward and clipping it via its own `overflow: hidden` — visible as the whole layout floating high with a dead gap at the bottom where the shifted content got clipped. Reported as "the AI chat box is floating high" on both the demo and a managed-hosting instance, but not on a personal instance whose browser window happened to be tall enough that the sidebar never overflowed in the first place — same code everywhere, the trigger is just how much sidebar content plus how tall the window is, which is why it read as instance-specific at first. **Fix**: added `block: 'nearest', inline: 'nearest'` to both `scrollIntoView` calls — `'nearest'` only scrolls a container if the target isn't already visible within it, so an already-fully-visible outer ancestor is left alone regardless of what any inner sidebar is doing. **A related, previously-latent gap surfaced by the same investigation**: even after that fix, the sidebar's module `<nav>` still had no scroll of its own (`flex-1` with no `min-h-0`/`overflow-y-auto`) — on a short window with enough modules enabled, Settings/Help/Sign out could end up genuinely unreachable with nothing to scroll them into view, since the outer shell wasn't meant to be user-scrollable at all. Fixed by giving that `<nav>` its own `min-h-0 overflow-y-auto`, the same pattern `<main>` already uses elsewhere in this exact file — the header, Pinned section, and footer (Settings/Help/Sign out/collapse) stay fixed in place while only the module list scrolls internally. **How to apply:** any `scrollIntoView()` call in this codebase should default to including `block: 'nearest'` (and `inline: 'nearest'` for horizontal cases) unless there's a specific reason to force top/center alignment — the plain no-options call is a footgun the instant there's more than one scrollable ancestor between the target and the viewport, and this app's fixed-shell architecture (multiple nested `overflow` regions, per the mobile-keyboard saga above) means that's often true here. **Verified**: reproduced cold in a from-scratch isolated backend + fresh Playwright browser context (a byte-identical production build to what was actually running on the affected demo instance, confirmed by matching hashed asset filenames) — confirmed the outer shell's `scrollTop` was nonzero before the fix and stayed `0` after across both fixes; confirmed the sidebar's module list independently scrolls to reveal Settings/Help while the header stays pinned at `top: 0`. Full backend suite unaffected (774 green, frontend-only change).

**`eslint <dir>` with no `--ext` flag silently limits directory traversal to `.js` — a whole `.jsx` tree can pass CI lint for years without ever being checked (2026-08-14):** `package.json`'s `"lint": "eslint src/ --max-warnings 0"` had run this way since the project began. Under ESLint 8's eslintrc config, giving `eslint` a *directory* target (as opposed to explicit file paths) walks only files matching the default `--ext` list, which is `.js` alone unless overridden — this repo has 6 `.js` files and 92 `.jsx` files, meaning CI's lint step had been silently skipping virtually the entire React component tree the whole time. Discovered while migrating to ESLint 9's flat config, which doesn't have this failure mode at all (file matching is an explicit glob *inside* the config, e.g. `files: ['src/**/*.{js,jsx}']`, not a CLI flag) — migrating correctly and turning the .jsx tree on for the first time surfaced 1489 previously-invisible problems in one shot (1417 were `react/prop-types`, confirming PropTypes were never adopted anywhere in this codebase — a real, established convention once checked, not oversight, so turned off explicitly rather than either reintroducing the blind spot or starting a ~90-component PropTypes-writing project; the other 72 were real and fixed — see `eslint.config.js`'s own comments for the specific `react-hooks/exhaustive-deps` cases left deliberately suppressed rather than "fixed" because blindly adding a missing dependency would have changed real behavior, e.g. turned a mount-once fetch into a refetch loop). **How to apply:** any time a lint/type-check/test command targets a *directory* rather than an explicit file list or a config-owned glob, verify what extensions or patterns it's actually walking — a command that "passes" because it silently checked nothing is worse than one that fails loudly, and the gap can persist indefinitely since a passing CI badge gives no signal that anything is wrong. Prefer flat-config-style explicit globs (config owns the file-matching) over CLI extension flags (caller must remember them) wherever the tool supports it.

**The repo's own `brain/` directory is the real running Docker instance's live data, not a clean template — don't `cp -r` it for an "isolated instance" test (2026-08-12):** `docker-compose.yml` mounts `../brain:/data/brain`, so the `logcore-app` container running on the dev machine reads/writes this exact repo-relative path. `brain/_system/` is gitignored (never committed) but very much populated locally with real accounts once the app has been used — a `cp -r brain/ /tmp/...` to set up an isolated verification instance copies that real data (caught before any writes happened: the copy was made, but every subsequent API call against it correctly failed for unrelated reasons before touching it further, and the copy was deleted immediately on discovery). **How to apply:** for an isolated verification brain, build the skeleton from just the git-tracked pieces — `mkdir _system` (empty; the app creates `auth.json`/`features.json` on first write) + `cp -r brain/USERS/_template` (the only part of `brain/USERS/` that's actually generic and git-tracked; `git ls-files brain/USERS/_template` confirms) — never copy `brain/` wholesale. Same reasoning as the established "verified in an isolated instance, never the real family data" practice elsewhere in this file — this is the mechanism by which that rule can be violated by accident even when the intent is already to isolate.

**`new Date().toISOString()` is UTC, not local — never use it to get "today" as a calendar date (2026-08-12, `Tasks.jsx`/`Goals.jsx`):** `TaskCard` and `GoalCard`'s own "is this overdue" check each computed `today` via `new Date().toISOString().split('T')[0]`. `toISOString()` always renders in UTC regardless of the browser's timezone, so for anyone west of UTC (e.g. America/Chicago, UTC-5/-6) the "today" string silently rolls over to tomorrow's date hours before local midnight — 7 PM local during CDT, 6 PM during CST. Any task/goal due that same day then satisfied `due_date < today` and showed a red "OVERDUE" badge hours early. The correct pattern already existed in five other places in this codebase (`Household.jsx`, `Team.jsx`, `Calendar.jsx`, `CalendarGrid.jsx`, `finance/money.js`, plus `Tasks.jsx`'s and `Goals.jsx`'s own top-level filter-tab logic) — a `todayStr()`-shaped helper using `d.getFullYear()`/`getMonth()`/`getDate()` (local getters). `TaskCard`/`GoalCard` were the only two call sites that diverged from the established pattern, both by locally recomputing "today" inside a child component instead of using the parent's already-correct value. **Fix**: both card components now take `today` as a prop from their parent (mirroring how `Household.jsx`/`Team.jsx` already did it) instead of recomputing it. **How to apply:** never reach for `.toISOString()`/`.toISOString().slice()` when you want "today" or "this month" as a human-facing calendar value — it's correct only for a true UTC timestamp (e.g. an API payload field), never for a same-day comparison. If a parent component already computes "today" correctly, thread it down as a prop rather than letting a child recompute its own copy — a second implementation of the same "what day is it" logic is exactly where this kind of drift creeps back in. **Verified live**: reproduced and fixed in a window where the bug was actively triggering (8 PM CDT, machine clock already showing Aug 13 UTC) — screenshotted a task/goal due "today" in an isolated Playwright-driven browser session before and reasoned through the exact UTC-vs-local values, confirming the badge does not show after the fix at the exact moment it would have before.

**A value normalized at write time needs the same normalization at every later lookup — and frontend state after a mutating call must come from the server's response, not the pre-request local value (2026-08-12, `routers/features.py` custom feature roles):** `CreateRoleRequest` strip+lowercases a new role's name before storing it as a dict key (`"Cleaner"` → `"cleaner"`), but `update_role`/`delete_role` (role name as a path param) and `set_user_feature_role` (role name in the request body — the actual "assign this role to a user" endpoint) all did an exact-match dict lookup with no normalization of their own. Any of the three 404/400'd when called with a differently-cased or padded name than what was actually persisted — most visibly right after creating a role, since `RoleDefinitions.jsx`'s `createRole()` compounded it by updating its own local state from the *locally typed* name instead of the create response's canonical `name`/`modules`, so the UI would keep showing (and re-submitting) the wrong-cased key until a page refresh. **Fix**: normalize at all four sites that key off a role name (the three above + `admin_create_user` in `routers/auth.py`, which persists a caller-supplied `feature_role` onto a new user with no validation at all); frontend now trusts the server's response over its own pre-request copy. **How to apply:** any time a Pydantic validator or service function canonicalizes a value on the way in (trim/lowercase/slugify), grep for every other place that same field is looked up by exact match and confirm it normalizes identically — and after any create/update call, seed local UI state from the response body, not from the values that were sent, since the two may not survive the round trip unchanged. Covered by `tests/test_features.py` (previously zero coverage on this file — see `docs/TESTING.md`).

**A resolved `pending_write`/`pending_question`/`pending_plan` step must be dropped on resume, not carried forward — found live in production, 2026-08-10, one day after the mechanism itself shipped:** `run_agent`'s resume path seeded `steps` from the *entire* persisted pending-turn record (`steps: list[dict] = list((resume or {}).get("steps", []))`), which still contained the original pending step being resolved right now. Replaying it appended the real `tool_call` on top but never removed the stale pending entry — so a run that genuinely *completed* (no further tool calls needed, `status: "agent"`) still returned a `steps` array containing a `pending_write`. The frontend's `pendingWrites = m.steps?.filter(s => s.type === 'pending_write')` check doesn't cross-reference `mode`, so it rendered a second, phantom `ApprovalCard` for an already-finished task. That phantom card's `runId` was `undefined` (a completed response never carries a `run_id` — only `awaiting_approval`/`awaiting_answer` do), and clicking it sent `resume: {run_id: undefined, ...}`, which `JSON.stringify` silently drops — the API correctly 422'd with "field required" on the now-missing `run_id`. Owner's exact repro: approve once (real change lands), get asked to approve *again*, approve that too, get the "field required" error. **Fix**: filter `pending_write`/`pending_question`/`pending_plan` out of the seeded `steps` before replaying — a resolved pause is not an open one. **How to apply:** when a data structure is both "the running trace of what happened" and "the signal for what's still open," resolving an item has to remove it from the open-signal role explicitly — appending its resolution isn't enough, since old and new readers of the same field can't tell which entries are still live without a change in shape (here, `mode`) that one of the two readers wasn't actually checking. **Verified**: two new tests (`test_agent_approve_mode.py`) — a single resume that completes has zero `pending_write` entries left in its `steps`; a *genuine* second write still pauses correctly (real `run_id` carried through, same `run_id` throughout the chain) and resolving that one too leaves nothing stale either. Full suite 596 green. Redeployed same-day.

**`ChatRequest.history` must alternate user/assistant *in the frontend's own local state too*, not just server-side — `sendResume()` broke it by appending instead of replacing (found live in production, 2026-08-10, same day as the pause/resume mechanism that introduced it):** Approving a pending write called `setMessages(prev => [...prev, newAssistantMsg])`, but the pending-write message it was "resolving" was *already* the last entry in `messages` — appending left two consecutive assistant turns. The very next `/chat` call (another resume, or a brand-new message) built its `history` from that same `messages` array and got rejected: `ValueError: History message N has unexpected role 'assistant'` (`ChatRequest`'s `validate_history_alternates`). Local state never self-heals, so every send for the rest of that chat session failed the same way — exactly the owner's report ("any new message didnt work in the chat after that"). **Fix**: `sendResume()` now replaces the last message (`prev.slice(0, -1)` + the new one) instead of appending — the pending turn and its resolution are the same logical turn across two round trips, not two turns. **How to apply:** whenever a UI "completes" or "resolves" an already-rendered provisional message (a pending card, a streaming placeholder, anything with its own later-arriving final state), replace that array entry in place; appending a second entry for the same turn silently corrupts any downstream logic — server or client — that assumes one array position per conversational turn. **Verified**: reproduced the exact broken sequence in an isolated instance by inspecting the actual outgoing request body (`history: ["user","assistant","assistant"]`, matching the reported error) before the fix, confirmed `["user","assistant"]` after it, across all four resume paths (approve/decline, ask_user_question, propose_plan) since they all share `sendResume()`. Redeployed same-day.

**A default parameter that resets state on every call is a trap the moment that function gets a second caller with different needs — `Dashboard.jsx::loadCurrent()`, 2026-08-09:** `loadCurrent()` unconditionally called `setEditing(false)` — correct for its original one caller (the initial-load/dashboard-switch effect, where dropping back to view mode is exactly right) but wrong for `saveBlocks()`, which also calls it to refresh state after every add/edit/remove block. The result: adding a single block silently exited Edit mode, hiding the whole toolbar (+Add Block/Save Layout/Settings) and every block's ✎/✕ chrome, forcing a re-click of "Edit Dashboard" after each addition. Fixed with an opt-out param (`resetEditing = true`, so the original caller needs no change) passed `false` from every mid-edit call site: `saveBlocks`, `onBlockAction`, and the Access/Settings modals' `onSaved`. **How to apply:** when a shared helper's side effect is only correct for *some* callers, make it an explicit, named parameter with the majority-correct default — not a silent behavior every new caller has to remember to work around.

**`y: Infinity` as a "place at the bottom" sentinel doesn't survive `JSON.stringify` — it silently becomes `null` (2026-08-09):** `Dashboard.jsx::addBlock()` set a new block's default layout to `{y: Infinity, ...}`, intending react-grid-layout to auto-place it — but that layout object is also what gets sent straight to `PATCH /dashboards/{id}` as the block's *stored* layout, and `JSON.stringify(Infinity)` produces `null`, not a number. The server was persisting a literal `null` row for every newly-added block. It happened to render fine (react-grid-layout tolerates a missing/null `y` gracefully) — a purely latent data-quality bug, never visibly broken — but confirmed genuinely present on real data (3 already-affected blocks in one real user's `dashboards.json`, found via `grep '"y": *null'`; left as-is per owner decision rather than backfilled with a migration, since they already render correctly). **Fixed with a real computed integer**: `Dashboard.jsx` gained `nextY(blocks, breakpoint)` (max of existing blocks' `y + h` per breakpoint, default 0) — exactly mirroring `dashboards_service.py`'s own `stacked_layout()` (renamed from `_stacked_layout` the same day so `agent_service.py`'s new `add_dashboard_block` tool can reuse the identical server-side computation, rather than risk an independent, possibly-divergent reimplementation of the same "place at the bottom" math). **How to apply:** never rely on `Infinity`/`NaN`/`undefined` surviving a JSON round-trip as a sentinel — compute the real value before it can be serialized, and if the same computation is needed on both sides of a client/server boundary, share one implementation rather than writing it twice.

**Dashboard grid units are baked into every saved dashboard's block layout — changing `cols`/`rowHeight` in `DashboardGrid.jsx` requires a rescale migration, not just a config edit (2026-08-05):**
`react-grid-layout` stores each block's position/size as plain integers (`x`, `y`, `w`, `h`) in grid-cell units, and those numbers are persisted verbatim in the dashboard's JSON — there's no unit metadata alongside them. When the grid moved from 12 cols/80px rows to 36 cols/24px rows (finer positioning/resizing, owner request), every already-saved block's numbers would have been silently reinterpreted against the new grid and rendered as tiny slivers clustered in the top-left corner. Fixed with a one-time migration (`m011_rescale_dashboard_grid_units` in `migrations/runner.py`) that multiplies every stored block's `layout.lg`/`layout.sm` `x/y/w/h` by the same scale factor (3) applied to the grid itself, across every store via `dashboards_service._all_stores()` (not `m010`'s narrower per-user-workspace loop, which skips the `_household`/`_team` pool stores entirely). **Not idempotent by design** (a second run would scale again, 9× total) — same accepted-risk shape as every other migration here, correctness depends on `migrations.json` never being hand-edited/reset. **How to apply:** any future change to `cols`/`rowHeight` in `DashboardGrid.jsx` needs a matching migration, and the three other places that hardcode the current grid's units in a fallback (`Dashboard.jsx::addBlock()`, `DashboardGrid.jsx`'s own missing-layout fallback, `dashboards_service.py::_validate_blocks()`) all need to move together or a block missing a saved layout renders at the wrong scale.

**Dashboard grid deliberately disables `react-grid-layout`'s default auto-compaction and collision-push behavior (2026-08-05):** `compactType={null}` and `preventCollision={true}` on `DashboardGrid.jsx`'s `<ResponsiveGridLayout>` are intentional, not oversights — the library's defaults (vertical auto-compact + push-neighbors-on-collision) actively fight "place a block at any point, including snug side-by-side with another, and have it stay exactly there," which was an explicit owner requirement, not an assumed side effect of a finer grid. **Trade-off accepted**: removing a block leaves a permanent gap (no auto-fill), and swapping two blocks' positions takes two separate moves instead of one auto-displacing the other. If a future request wants auto-tidying back, that's a direct reversion of this decision, not a bug fix.

**CONFIRMED AND FIXED, 2026-08-05 — the ~558px-fixed-width grid container below was real, not a sandbox artifact.** Owner independently hit the exact symptom on their real device ("it only has 2 columns... only allows 2 width sizes") before this was fixed, which is precisely what happens when the grid container falls below the `lg` breakpoint's 768px threshold and `react-grid-layout` falls back to `cols.sm: 2`. Fix: `Dashboard.jsx`'s root `<div>` changed from `className="max-w-5xl mx-auto"` to `className="w-full max-w-5xl mx-auto"`. The one-word addition matters because of the exact CSS mechanism described below — `width:auto` (the implicit default) is what triggers the auto-margin-consumes-everything behavior; `w-full` gives the item a definite cross-size, so `max-width` can still cap it while `mx-auto` correctly centers only the genuine leftover space. Verified post-fix across three viewport widths (900/1100/1920px) that the container width now scales proportionally and caps at exactly 1024px, instead of sticking at ~558px regardless of viewport size.

**Original finding (kept for the CSS mechanism explanation) — dashboard grid container measured suspiciously narrow (~558px, fixed regardless of viewport width) in an isolated headless-Chromium test:** While verifying the grid rescale above, an isolated-backend + synthetic-brain + Playwright check (same methodology as the scroll-containment entries below) measured `.dashboard-grid`'s `getBoundingClientRect().width` at ~558px at viewport widths of 1000/1400/1920px — i.e., it never grows past ~558px no matter how much space is available, which would put every dashboard on the "sm" (2-col, mobile-stacked) `react-grid-layout` breakpoint instead of the intended 36-col desktop grid even on a normal-width desktop window. Root-caused (not just observed) to CSS, not React logic: `Dashboard.jsx`'s root `<div className="max-w-5xl mx-auto space-y-4">` sits inside `Layout.jsx`'s `<main className="... flex flex-col">` — a flex item with **auto margins on the cross axis of a column-flex container does not stretch under `align-items: stretch`**, it shrink-to-fits instead (a real, if obscure, CSS flexbox rule), and `WidthProvider`'s measured wrapper has no intrinsic content width of its own since `react-grid-layout` positions every item with `position: absolute` (removed from normal flow, contributing nothing to a shrink-to-fit calculation) — so the whole chain collapses to whatever an unrelated sibling (the header row or the GettingStarted card) happens to need. **Neither `Dashboard.jsx`'s root className nor `Layout.jsx`'s `<main>` was touched this session** — this structure predates the grid-rescale work entirely, so if it's real it already affected the original 12-col grid too (just less visibly, since chunkier default block sizes made the collapsed width look plausible rather than obviously wrong). It was also never reported by the owner after the 2026-08-04 Custom Dashboards ship, and the isolated test environment had a directly-observed, unrelated font-load failure (`fonts.gstatic.com` unreachable, no internet in the sandbox) — a very plausible cause for a shrink-to-fit measurement to stick at a pre-font-swap value. **Not fixed, not confirmed live** — flagging per this file's own established pattern for layout issues that are "structurally invisible" to headless Chromium (see the three iOS-PWA entries below): open the real Dashboard on a real desktop browser and check whether blocks actually get the full ~1024px-capped width, or whether they're stuck narrow. If narrow, the fix is almost certainly on `Dashboard.jsx`'s root div (e.g., drop `mx-auto` in favor of `w-full` + a separate centering wrapper, or set an explicit `width: 100%` on it) — not `DashboardGrid.jsx` itself, and not the grid-unit rescale, both of which are unrelated to this container-width mechanism.


**`origin` is HTTPS (for cron) but pushing needs the SSH deploy key against the SSH URL directly, and parallel sessions can diverge local from origin (2026-07-31):**
Owner's PC host had this repo's `master` diverged from `origin/master` — a local unpushed commit (the 2026-07-30 Settings/Admin restructure) sat next to a commit another parallel Claude Code session had pushed straight to `origin/master` (a `docs/TASKS.md`-only commit, per-session commit attribution: `Claude-Session:` trailer). `git status` reported "have diverged." Both edits touched `docs/TASKS.md` but in non-overlapping regions, so `git merge origin/master` resolved with zero conflicts. Pushing then failed with `git push origin master` → `fatal: could not read Username for 'https://github.com'`: `origin`'s fetch URL is intentionally HTTPS (see the entry below, 2026-07-20) so the updater's cron user can fetch without an SSH agent, but that means there's no stored credential for an interactive push either. Fix: push straight to the SSH URL without changing `origin` — `GIT_SSH_COMMAND="ssh -i /home/logcore/.ssh/logcore_github" git push git@github.com:LogCoreTech/LogCoreOS.git master` (the deploy key `logcore_github` already has push access; confirmed via `ssh -T git@github.com`). **How to apply:** before assuming a "GitHub sync issue" is the old cron/SSH-remote bug, run `git fetch origin master` + `git status` first — a real branch divergence from a parallel session looks completely different and needs a merge/rebase, not a remote-URL fix. `docs/AGENTS.md`'s Git Workflow section documented the now-stale `git push origin master` form; updated to the SSH-URL form here.

**`installed_version.json` is the ONLY runtime version source — and the host clone must be fetchable by the updater's cron user (2026-07-20):**
The app's "current version" (Admin → Updates) comes solely from `brain/_system/installed_version.json`, stamped by `update.sh`/`launch.sh` — the repo/image `VERSION` file is never read at runtime, so the two can disagree. The v0.4.1 update "succeeded" while still showing 0.4.0 because the host clone's origin was the SSH remote (`git@github.com:`) and the cron environment had no key: `git fetch` failed, pre-hardening `update.sh` ignored the failure, rebuilt the unchanged tree, and stamped the old VERSION as a successful update. `update.sh` now aborts with status `fetch-failed` on fetch errors, verifies the version stamp landed (`success-stamp-failed` otherwise), and re-stamps a lagging version on no-op runs. Deployment rule: point host clones at the public HTTPS remote (`git remote set-url origin https://github.com/LogCoreTech/LogCoreOS.git`) unless a cron-readable deploy key is installed. Also remember `do_update` is invoked as `do_update || …`, which disables `set -e` inside the whole function — every step needs an explicit exit-code check. Release checklist for version-bearing files: root `VERSION` (authoritative), `CHANGELOG.md`, `help.json` `whats_new`, and `app/frontend/package.json` + `package-lock.json` (cosmetic but visible in build logs; was stuck at 0.1.0 until 0.4.2). **As of 0.4.3 updates are ATOMIC**: `update.sh` installs exactly the commit the latest published GitHub release tag points at (not master's tip), so publishing the release is both the deploy trigger and the content selector — commits on master after a release do not ship until the next release. `UPDATE_CHANNEL=edge` in `docker/.env` restores master-tip tracking for dev boxes. Master no longer needs to be ship-ready at every moment, but the tagged commit does.

**iOS standalone PWA mode renders as if a phantom browser toolbar is still pushing the page up — `html` needs deliberately-oversized `min-height` to absorb it (2026-07-20, recurred four times, root cause found + CONFIRMED FIXED on-device 2026-07-29):**
`Layout.jsx`'s mobile footer nav failed "floats above the true screen bottom, exposing background below it" through FIVE separate fixes. The first four all verified clean in Chromium and still failed on the owner's real device (confirmed after a full PWA delete + reinstall each time, ruling out client-side caching):
1. `h-[100dvh]` → `h-full` + explicit `#root { height: 100% }` (CHANGELOG [0.4.1]) — assumed the height-*resolution* chain was the problem.
2. Footer switched to `position: fixed; bottom: 0` (still nested inside Layout's `overflow-hidden` flex containers) — should be immune to (1) by spec. Still failed.
3. Footer (+ module drawer) moved to render as top-level siblings outside *every* `overflow-hidden` ancestor in `Layout.jsx` — targeting the real Safari bug where `fixed` clips to the nearest `overflow: hidden` ancestor's box. Still failed, because the nearest remaining such ancestor was `body` itself.
4. `body` switched from `height: 100%` to `position: fixed; inset: 0` (bulletproof viewport anchor, verified via Playwright computed styles) and `background-attachment: fixed` removed (unreliable on iOS, and pointless since `body` never scrolls) — motivated by the owner noticing the exposed gap tracked light/dark mode but not the custom background gradient. Owner then explicitly tested a colorful gradient in dark mode: **still didn't reach that spot.** Still failed.
5. **Actual root cause, found by asking the one question all four fixes skipped**: does this happen in a regular Safari tab too, or only the installed home-screen PWA? Owner confirmed: **regular browser tab renders it correctly; only the standalone PWA is broken.** Since none of `Layout.jsx`'s or `index.css`'s code differs between the two modes, every prior CSS theory was necessarily wrong — the bug isn't in the app's layout code at all, it's iOS's own standalone-mode rendering. This matches a documented WebKit bug (Apple Developer Forums thread 744327; see also the "Make Your PWAs Look Handsome on iOS" writeup) where standalone PWAs render as if an invisible browser toolbar is still present and pushes the whole page upward — content ends up short of the true bottom edge by roughly the top safe-area amount, exactly matching this symptom, and exactly why a plain Safari tab (which has its own real toolbar taking that role) never shows it.

**Fix (CONFIRMED on-device — owner verified the footer sits flush with no gap, 2026-07-29):** `index.css` — `html` gets `min-height: calc(100% + env(safe-area-inset-top))` (taller than the true viewport on purpose) alongside its existing `height: 100%`; `body` reverted to the simple `height: 100%` chain (the field-tested pattern for this bug doesn't use `position: fixed` on `body` — that was attempt 4's now-superseded theory). The extra height gives the phantom upward shift somewhere to go (into the padding/overflow-hidden buffer) instead of shorting the real content. **How to apply:** if a mobile-only layout bug reproduces identically across every "should definitely work" CSS fix, and especially if a `position: fixed; bottom: 0` element with zero remaining `overflow-hidden` ancestors still won't sit flush — stop changing CSS and check whether it's standalone-PWA-specific first (same URL in a plain browser tab vs. the installed home-screen icon). That single test collapses an enormous space of wrong theories immediately, and this class of bug plus its exact workaround is externally documented, not something to rediscover from first principles. **This class of bug still cannot be verified in Chromium** — every attempt "worked" there and failed on-device regardless; only a real device (or, as here, asking the right diagnostic question) closes it out.

**Follow-up, same file, different symptom — `overflow: hidden` alone doesn't stop the whole document from touch-scrolling on iOS standalone PWA (2026-08-03, fix applied, NOT yet confirmed on-device):**
While building the first two "fixed-shell, only inner content scrolls" pages (`Chat.jsx`, then `Journal.jsx` — every prior page was a normal scrolling page where this was never exercised), the owner reported that on the installed iPhone PWA the *entire* app shell scrolls together, including the mobile top header (`Layout.jsx`'s `<header>`, which sits **outside** `<main>` and structurally should never move regardless of what scrolls inside `main`) — only the `position: fixed` bottom nav stayed anchored. A from-scratch Playwright/Chromium test (isolated backend + synthetic brain, real device not touched) proved `main`'s own containment is correct — zero overflow measured on `main`, page root, or inner panels at both mobile and desktop sizes, even with a 40-line entry forcing real internal scroll. That rules out the app's own flex/overflow logic and points at the browser: iOS Safari (confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` — the owner is on the installed home-screen PWA specifically) has a long-documented quirk where `overflow: hidden` on `html`/`body` does not reliably prevent a touch-drag from rubber-banding the whole document, independent of the phantom-toolbar bug above. The standard, widely-documented fix is `position: fixed` on `body`, which removes it from the normal scrolling flow entirely (a fixed element has nothing for a touch-scroll gesture to grab).

**This directly touches attempt 4 above, which tried exactly `body { position: fixed; inset: 0 }` and was reverted** — but re-reading that history: attempt 4 was dropped because it didn't fix *that* bug (the footer gap), not because it caused a new one; the team just found a simpler fix (html's oversized `min-height`) and moved on. It was never established that `position: fixed` on `body` is harmful, only that it wasn't the tool for that particular job. Applied now with one deliberate difference from attempt 4: `top: 0; left: 0; right: 0` instead of `inset: 0` (which also implies `bottom: 0`) — `height: 100%` is kept, so body's height still resolves against `html`'s deliberately-oversized height rather than being clamped to the raw viewport, preserving the mechanism the footer fix depends on. Re-verified via the same isolated Playwright setup that this doesn't regress normal scrolling pages (`Tasks` at both mobile and desktop sizes) or the new fixed-layout pages (`Journal`) — `main` still shows zero unwanted overflow everywhere. **What Playwright/Chromium cannot verify**: whether this actually stops the iOS touch-scroll bug (it doesn't reproduce there at all, same limitation as the entry above), and whether it reintroduces the footer gap on a real device now that `body` is `position: fixed` again. **Needs on-device confirmation of BOTH**: (1) the whole-shell-scrolls bug is gone, (2) the footer still sits flush with no gap. If (2) regresses, the interaction between this fix and `html`'s oversized `min-height` is the first thing to re-examine — they were designed independently and this is their first time coexisting.

**Third round on the same bug class — owner reported it app-wide (Settings worst), not just Chat/Journal; hardened with `overscroll-behavior` + closed a real, separate textarea-flex bug found on the way (2026-08-04, fix applied, NOT yet confirmed on-device):**
Owner reported "most all pages scroll the background... Settings is the biggest issue" — broader than the app-wide fix above, which targeted the whole-shell-scrolls symptom specifically. Re-ran the same isolated-backend + synthetic-brain + Playwright methodology as the two entries above, this time crawling all ~27 authenticated routes (not just Chat/Journal) at both mobile and desktop viewports. First pass falsely showed every route redirecting to `/login` on the second (desktop) viewport pass — root cause was the *test*, not the app: `page.goto()` forces a full hard reload per route (unlike real users' client-side `<Link>` navigation), and 27 back-to-back reloads exhausted the `GET /auth/me` rate limit (30/min) right as the desktop pass started, so `AuthProvider`'s `refreshUser()` 429'd, `sessionChecked` still flipped `true` in its `finally`, and the app correctly-but-misleadingly rendered as logged-out. Paced the crawler (1.6s/route, 65s gap between viewport passes) and re-ran clean: **zero `html`/`body` overflow on any route, either viewport** — confirming, again, that this bug class is real but structurally invisible to Chromium, consistent with both entries above. Applied hardening anyway rather than concluding "nothing to fix," on the reasoning that `Settings` being called out as worst is what you'd expect if the *existing* fixes are real but incomplete under real touch input specifically (Settings pages are simply the ones with by far the most vertical content, so they're the ones where any residual scroll-escape would actually be visible — every Settings page uses the exact same plain `max-w-lg mx-auto space-y-6` structure as pages that weren't flagged, so there's no Settings-specific code bug to find, only more content to trigger one).

**Fix, layered on top of the existing `position: fixed` body/html defense**: added `overscroll-behavior: none` to both `html` and `body`, and `overscroll-y-contain` to `<main>` (`Layout.jsx`) — this is the modern, purpose-built CSS property for exactly this failure mode (stops a touch-drag's rubber-band/bounce from chaining up past a scroll container once it hits its boundary), complementing rather than replacing the `position: fixed` technique, which only addresses *whether* body itself can scroll, not whether an inner scroller's overscroll can still leak upward. No prior entry in this saga had tried `overscroll-behavior` — worth noting for the next person who finds this doc mid-debugging so they don't re-derive it from scratch.

**A genuinely separate, real bug found while auditing (not the same bug, same *class*)**: `Brain.jsx`'s file editor and `Notes.jsx`'s note editor both had a `<textarea>` set directly as a flex item (`className="flex-1 ..."`) with no `min-h-0` wrapper — the exact pattern the Journal fixed-layout entry below already identified and fixed *there* on 2026-08-03 ("form controls get special-cased in the flex sizing algorithm... plain divs are safe as the flex-1 scrolling item; form controls are not — wrap them") but which was never back-ported to these two older editors. Fixed both the same way Journal was: wrap the `<textarea>` in a plain `<div className="flex-1 min-h-0 overflow-hidden">`, textarea itself becomes `w-full h-full` + `overscroll-contain`. Also swapped both editors' `100vh`-based height calcs to `100dvh` (mobile browser chrome resize safety — already the reasoning behind `.modal-card`'s `max-height: 85dvh` in this same file; `100vh` doesn't shrink when the address bar is showing, `100dvh` does). **How to apply**: any future fixed-height editor panel (calc-based or flex-based) should be checked for both of these — a raw `<textarea>` as a direct flex-1 child, and `vh` instead of `dvh` in a height calc — since this is now the *second* time the same textarea mistake has been found independently in different files, it's worth grepping for `className="flex-1.*textarea\|<textarea[^>]*flex-1` (or eyeballing every `<textarea` for a `flex-1` in its own class) the next time this bug class comes up again rather than waiting for a third report.

**Also closed in the same pass**: every authenticated page missing the established mobile-footer-clipping reinforcement spacer (`<div className="h-20 md:hidden" aria-hidden="true" />`, first introduced 2026-08-03 for Contacts and generalized to 5 more pages same day) — 23 pages had never gotten it, essentially the entire Settings tree (`Settings.jsx` + all 5 `settings/*.jsx` + all 9 `settings/admin/*.jsx`) plus `Assets`, `Automations`, `Finance`, `Goals`, `Help`, `Home`, `Team`, and `Brain`'s file-list view. `Notes.jsx`'s split sidebar/editor layout was deliberately left without the blanket spacer (too structurally different — negative-margin breakout, conditional sidebar/editor toggle — to risk a blind copy-paste without on-device verification); `Profile.jsx` didn't need one directly since it already inherits the spacer from `ContactDetail.jsx`/`ContactModal.jsx`'s existing `fullPage` prop handling.

**What Playwright/Chromium still cannot verify** (same structural limitation as both entries above): whether `overscroll-behavior` actually closes the touch-rubber-band gap on a real iOS standalone PWA, and whether the Settings pages specifically now clear the footer nav on-device. Needs on-device confirmation before considering this closed — flag to the owner as the next real-world test, same as every fix in this saga.

**No keyboard-aware viewport handling existed anywhere in the app — the keyboard panned the visual viewport instead of resizing layout, and the pan didn't always reset cleanly on dismiss (2026-08-14, fix applied, NOT yet confirmed on-device):**
Reported symptom: after the on-screen keyboard opens then closes, tapping something lands roughly 3 inches off from where it visually appears — displayed content reverted correctly, touch/scroll coordinate mapping did not. This app's scroll architecture deliberately takes `<body>` out of normal flow (`position: fixed`, `overflow: hidden` on `html`/`body` — see the iOS PWA sagas above) with `<main>` as the only real scroll container, built around `100dvh`/flex `min-h-0`. The viewport meta tag never specified `interactive-widget` at all, and zero `visualViewport` listener/resize handler existed anywhere in the app. Absent `interactive-widget=resizes-content`, the keyboard pans the *visual* viewport (to keep the focused input visible) rather than resizing actual page layout — a documented WebKit/PWA bug class where that pan offset doesn't always reset cleanly on dismiss in standalone PWA mode, matching the symptom exactly.
**Fix (two-layered, same pattern as the iOS PWA saga above — primary CSS/meta fix plus a defensive JS backstop):** (1) `index.html`'s viewport meta tag gained `interactive-widget=resizes-content`, so the keyboard resizes layout (affecting `100dvh`) instead of just panning — consistent with how this app's scroll architecture already works everywhere else. (2) `Layout.jsx` gained a `visualViewport` `resize` listener that forces `window.scrollTo(0, 0)` when the visual viewport height returns to matching the full layout height (keyboard closed) — a no-op when nothing was desynced, catches residual cases where (1) alone isn't sufficient.
**How to apply:** This is the same "structurally invisible to headless Chromium" class as the iOS PWA entries above — Playwright/Chromium cannot simulate the real WebKit keyboard-resize quirk, so this shipped unverified same as those. **Needs on-device confirmation**: open a text input, let the keyboard open and close, confirm taps land where they visually appear. If it regresses, check interaction with the `position: fixed` body/`overscroll-behavior` machinery those entries already established, since this is the first time keyboard-open/close specifically has been exercised against that architecture.

**Docker access goes through the socket-proxy (2026-07-20):**
The app no longer bind-mounts `/var/run/docker.sock`. It reaches the Docker API via the `socket-proxy` service (`DOCKER_HOST=tcp://socket-proxy:2375`, set in `docker-compose.yml`). `docker.from_env()` picks that up automatically — no code change. The proxy allows only `CONTAINERS`/`IMAGES`/`POST` (+ start/stop/restart); it blocks `EXEC`, `VOLUMES`, `NETWORKS`, etc. If you add a NEW Docker operation to the backend, check it falls within an allowed section or the call will 403 at the proxy. **`N8N_ENCRYPTION_KEY` is a data key** — never change it on an instance that already has n8n data (stored credentials become permanently undecryptable); `launch.sh` only generates it for fresh installs.

**Docker volume path for frontend dist:**
The backend resolves the frontend dist as `Path(__file__).parent.parent / "frontend" / "dist"`. Since `main.py` is at `/app/main.py` inside the container, `parent.parent` is `/`. The volume mount in `docker-compose.yml` must be:
```yaml
- ../app/frontend/dist:/frontend/dist   # correct
# NOT: ../app/frontend/dist:/app/frontend/dist  (breaks static file serving)
```

**Health check URL:**
The correct health check path is `/api/v1/health`. The old path `/api/health` does not exist.

**Docker socket permissions:**
The app user must be in the `docker` group. After `sudo usermod -aG docker <username>`, the user must log out and back in (or `newgrp docker`). In the current dev environment, use `echo "12345" | sudo -S docker <cmd>` if the group isn't active. Between 2026-07-11 and 2026-07-30 a Claude Code session in auto mode could not restart/rebuild Docker at all on this host — the docker socket was denied, `sg` wasn't installed, and piped-password sudo was blocked by the permission classifier. **As of 2026-07-30 this is no longer blocked**: `bash launch.sh` (no flags — full frontend build + image rebuild + restart) ran to completion unattended in an auto-mode session, self-elevating via a `sudo` re-launch (`launch.sh` handles this internally) with no owner intervention needed. Cause of the fix unconfirmed (host permission config likely changed independently of this codebase) — if a future session hits the old denial again, don't assume it's permanent; try the plain command first before concluding the owner has to run it.

**Host Python is 3.14 — run pytest from `app/backend/.venv` (uv-managed 3.12):**
A host OS upgrade (by 2026-07-11) removed Python 3.12, `pip`, `ensurepip`, and `sg`. The pinned backend deps (pydantic-core) don't build on 3.14. Dev/test env is now a uv-managed venv: `uv` lives at `~/.local/bin/uv`; the venv is `app/backend/.venv` (gitignored) created with `uv venv --python 3.12 .venv` + `uv pip install -r requirements.txt -r ../../requirements-dev.txt`. Run tests with `.venv/bin/pytest tests/ -v` and black with `.venv/bin/black`. The `docs/skills/run-tests/run.sh` bare `pytest` call won't work until PATH includes the venv.

**nvm and Node.js:**
nvm is a version manager — installing nvm does not install Node.js. Run `nvm install <version>` separately. Node.js is loaded via `.bashrc`; open a new terminal or source `.bashrc` before running `launch.sh`.

**Runtime hosting config vs env vars:**
`cookie_secure` and `trust_proxy_headers` can be in `docker/.env` (defaults) OR overridden at runtime by `brain/hosting.json`. The runtime value always wins. Never read `settings.*` directly in request-serving code.

**Backend code changes require Docker image rebuild:**
Python code is baked into the Docker image at build time. Any change to `app/backend/` requires:
```bash
bash launch.sh --skip-build   # rebuilds image only, skips npm
```
Running without `--reload` will not pick up file changes. **Stale-backend symptom signature** (frontend rebuilt, backend not): new routes return **405 Method Not Allowed** (the SPA catch-all is GET-only, so a PATCH/POST to a not-yet-deployed API path hits it), and requests to a changed Pydantic model return **422 "field required"** for fields the old model still marks required. Fix = rebuild the image. `launch.sh` elevates Docker via `sg docker` automatically (no sudo password needed when the user is in the docker group).

**Mobile viewport height — use `100dvh` not `100vh`:**
`100vh` on mobile includes browser chrome that appears/disappears while scrolling, making fixed elements overlap. Always use `h-[100dvh]` on the root container, not `h-screen` / `h-[100vh]`.

**Flex scroll containment — always add `min-h-0` to scrollable flex children:**
Without `min-h-0`, the browser defaults `min-height: auto`, letting the child grow to full content size and overflow its parent instead of scrolling. Required on the messages list in `Chat.jsx` and any other scrollable flex child. Never remove it.

**PWA standalone mode — `Cache-Control: no-cache` on `index.html`, NOT `no-store`:**
`no-store` prevents the browser from storing `index.html` at all, breaking iOS's ability to detect the page was saved to the home screen. The SPA catch-all in `main.py` must send `Cache-Control: no-cache`.

**httpOnly cookie timing on mobile login:**
After `POST /auth/login`, there is a timing gap before the cookie is available for subsequent requests on mobile. Never fire parallel requests immediately after login. Use the login response directly (it returns the same user object as `/me`) and then make follow-up calls sequentially.

**overflow-y-scroll on `<main>`, not overflow-y-auto:**
`<main>` in Layout.jsx uses `overflow-y-scroll` (not `auto`). With `auto`, the 4px scrollbar appears and disappears, shifting the content area by 4px and causing content sized for the full width to intermittently overflow. With `scroll`, the 4px is always reserved (transparent track, thumb only appears when needed), keeping layout stable. Also paired with `overflow-x-hidden` to prevent horizontal scroll.

**Never define React components inside other components:**
A component function defined inside another component (e.g. `function Foo() { function Bar() {...} }`) gets a new identity on every render of the outer component. React sees a new component type at the same tree position and unmounts + remounts it — dismissing keyboard focus, resetting state, etc. Always define sub-components at module level and pass state/handlers as props.

**brain/ ownership must match the app container user (uid 1000):**
The app container runs as `appuser` (uid/gid 1000). If `brain/` on the host is owned by anyone else (typical after a root clone on a VPS), the app crash-loops at startup with `PermissionError: [Errno 13] /data/brain/_system`. `launch.sh` fixes this automatically when run as root (`fix_brain_ownership()`); for other uid mismatches it prints the `chown -R 1000:1000 brain/` command. Symptom signature: `logcore-app: Restarting` + that PermissionError in `docker logs logcore-app`.

**Docker tunnel container conflict:**
If a container named `logcore-tunnel` is orphaned (from a crashed session), `launch.sh` will fail with a "container name already in use" conflict. Fix: `echo "12345" | sudo -S docker rm -f logcore-tunnel` then relaunch.

**A loopback-bound container port is still reachable through the Cloudflare tunnel:**
`docker-compose.yml`'s `tunnel` service runs with `network_mode: host`, so it shares the host's network namespace rather than Docker's internal bridge. That means `127.0.0.1:5680` (ntfy) or any other `127.0.0.1:<port>` bind on the host is reachable from *inside* the tunnel container — binding a service to loopback restricts direct LAN/internet access, but does **not** stop it from being exposed via a Cloudflare Public Hostname pointed at `http://localhost:<port>`. Don't assume "loopback-only" means "tunnel-inaccessible" when reasoning about exposure for any bundled service.

- **2026-08-27 — Dashboards converted into `module_packages/dashboard/` — the third LOCKED (`uninstallable: True`) module after Tasks and Chat, and structurally the most-depended-upon module converted so far, since every other module's own dashboard blocks read/write through it.** Owner, after confirming Notes' conversion had no cross-module data-safety issues: "commit and move on to the next module" — committed Notes, then proceeded directly into Dashboards per the tracked rollout order, same upfront research pass every increment gets (an Explore agent covering the full file inventory on both sides, which pieces of `components/dashboard/` are genuinely page-owned vs. shared rendering infrastructure, every cross-module consumer of `dashboards_service.py`, and the App.jsx home-route question specifically, since no prior conversion ever owned the root path).
  **A real naming decision needed upfront, not assumed from the plan doc's own directory name.** The rollout plan's own table calls this module "Dashboards" (plural, matching the target `module_packages/dashboard/` — singular — naming convention every other conversion follows for its OWN id).
